## (A) Highlights of listing requirements
### Track record and market capitalisation requirements
• Trading record of not less than three financial years
• Management continuity for at least the three preceding years and ownership continuity and control for at least the most recent audited financial year
#### • Three Financial Eligibility Tests:
##### i. Profit test
- Profits of HK$35 million for the most recent year and of aggregate of HK$45 million for the first two years
- Market capitalisation of at least HK$500 million at the time of listing

##### ii. Market capitalisation/revenue/cashflow test
- Revenue of at least HK$500 million for the most recent audited financial year
- Positive cashflow from operating activities of at least HK$100 million in aggregate for the three preceding financial years
- Market capitalisation of at least HK$2 billion at the time of listing

##### iii. Market capitalisation/revenue test

- Revenue of at least HK$500 million for the most recent audited financial year
- Market capitalisation of at least HK$4 billion at the time of listing
- Track record of less than three years may be accepted if:
	• Directors and management have sufficient and satisfactory experience of at least three years in the line of the listing business and industry
	• Management continuity for the most recent audited financial year

• Exemptions may be granted for mineral companies, newly formed project companies (such as major infrastructure projects) and pre-revenue/pre-profit biotech companies, which can have shorter trading records
• A focused line of business not specifically required, but its core business should meet the minimum financial requirement
• Must have control over its business

### Minimum public float
• Market capitalisation of at least HK$125 million held by the public at the time of listing
• Minimum 25% of the issuers’ total issued share capital at the time of listing
• The Exchange may, at its discretion, accept a percentage of public float between 15% and 25% if the market capitalisation of the issuer at the time of listing exceeds HK$10 billion

### Future prospects
• No specific requirement but the applicant is required to include a general statement of future plans and prospects
• Inclusion of profit forecast is optional

### Accountants’ report

• Must be prepared in accordance with Hong Kong Financial Reporting **Standards**, International Financial Reporting Standards, or the Accounting Standards for Business Enterprises of the People’s Republic of China (PRC CAS)
• Generally accepted accounting principles in the United States of America (**US GAAP) or other** accounting standards may be acceptable by the Exchange under certain circumstances
• In general, accountants’ report covers **at least three complete financial years**, immediately preceding the issue of the listing document
• The latest financial period reported on by the reporting accountants **must not have ended more than six months** before the date of the listing document

### Corporate governance
• At least **three independent non-executive directors** required
• Must appoint independent non-executive directors representing **at least one-third of the board**
• **Audit committee** required
• **Remuneration committee** required
• Required to appoint a **compliance adviser** for the period commencing on initial listing and ending on publication of financial results for the first full financial year after listing

### Acceptable jurisdictions
• Austria, Australia, Bermuda, Brazil, British Virgin Islands, Canada — Alberta, Canada — British Columbia, Canada — Ontario, Cayman Islands, Cyprus, England and Wales, France, Germany, Guernsey, Hong Kong, India, Ireland, Israel, Italy, Japan, Jersey, Labuan, Luxembourg, Netherlands, Russia, Singapore, Republic of Korea, The Isle of Man, The People’s Republic of China, United States of America — The State of California, United States of America — The State of Delaware, United States of America — The State of Nevada
• Other jurisdictions which are subject to appropriate standards of shareholder protection that are equivalent to those required under Hong Kong law may be considered
• Other jurisdictions for secondary listing will be considered

### Restrictions on controlling shareholders
• A controlling shareholder at the time of listing must undertake:
- Not to dispose of his interest in the issuer from the listing document issue date to the **first six months** after listing
- Not to dispose of his interest in the issuer in the **next six months** such that he would cease to be a controlling shareholder: at least 30% interest in the issuer

### Other considerations
• Competing businesses of controlling shareholders or directors are allowed but full disclosure is required
• Mandatory public offering of at least 10% of the total offer size
• New issue must be fully underwritten
• Listed issuer is not allowed to issue new securities in the first six months after listing

## (B) Specific listing requirements for pre-revenue/pre-profit biotech companies and issuers with weighted voting rights (WVR) structures

### Pre-revenue/ Pre-profit biotech companies

In addition to satisfying the general listing requirements (except for Financial Eligibility Tests) as highlighted in section (A), the pre-revenue/pre-profit biotech companies must meet the following requirements:

#### Features
• At least one core product beyond the concept stage
• Primarily engaged in research and development (R&D) for the core product(s) for at least 12 months prior to listing
• Has as primary reason for listing raising funds for R&D to bring the core product(s) to commercialisation
• Has registered patent(s), patent application(s) and/or intellectual property in relation to the core product(s)
• Received meaningful investment from at least one sophisticated investor at least 6 months before listing (which must remain at IPO)

#### Track record requirements 
• In current line of business for at least two financial years under substantially the same management

#### Market capitalisation and minimum public float requirements
• Market capitalisation of at least HK$1.5 billion at the time of listing
• Public float of at least 25% (with at least HK$375 million of public float at the time of listing, which is exclusion of subscriptions by existing shareholders at IPO and subscriptions through cornerstone investments)

#### Working capital requirement 
• Sufficient working capital which covers 125% of the group’s costs for at least 12 months from the date of publication of listing document

### Issuers with WVR structures
In addition to satisfying the general listing requirements as highlighted in section (A), the WVR issuers must meet the following requirements:

#### Features
• Innovative company which possesses more than one of the following characteristics:
- its success is attributable to application, to the company’s core business, of (i) new technologies, (ii) innovations and/or (iii) a new business model;
- R&D is a significant contributor of its expected value and constitutes a major activity and expense;
- its success is attributable to its unique features or intellectual property; and/or
- has an outsized market capitalisation/intangible asset value relative to its tangible asset value.

• Demonstrate a track record of high business growth and its high growth trajectory is expected to continue

• Each WVR beneficiary:
- has been materially responsible for the growth of the business, by way of his skills, knowledge and/or strategic direction; and
- must be (i) an individual who has an active executive role within the business, and has contributed to a material extent to the ongoing growth of the business; and (ii) a director of the issuer at the time of listing

• Received meaningful investment from at least one sophisticated investor (which must remain at IPO). Such investors must retain an aggregate 50% of their investment at the time of listing for a period of at least six months post-IPO

#### Market capitalisation requirement
• Market capitalisation of at least HK$10 billion at the time of listing and revenue of at least HK$1 billion in the most recent audited financial year; or
• Market capitalisation of at least HK$40 billion

#### Enhanced corporate governance

• Compliance adviser appointed on a permanent basis required
• Nomination committee comprising independent non-executive directors (INEDs) required
• INEDs subject to retirement by rotation at least once every three years
• Corporate governance committee comprising entirely of INEDs required
• Key matters decided on one-share one-vote basis
• WVR safeguards incorporated in constitutional documents

#### WVR safeguards

• Non-WVR shareholders hold at least 10% of voting power
• Voting power of WVR shares are not more than 10 times of the voting power of ordinary shares
• WVR beneficiaries collectively own at least 10% of the underlying economic interest in the applicant’s total issued share capital at the time of listing
• WVRs are restricted to directors and will lapse permanently if: (i) the beneficiary dies/ceases to be a director/is incapacitated/no longer meets the requirements of a director; or (ii) the shares are transferred to another person
• After listing, cannot increase the proportion of WVR in issue or issue any further WVR shares

## Hong Kong GEM

### Track record and market capitalisation requirements
• Trading record of not less than two financial years
• No profit requirement
• Positive cashflow generated from operating activities of at least HK$30 million in aggregate for the two preceding financial years
• Market capitalisation of at least HK$150 million at the time of listing
• Exemptions may be granted for mineral companies and newly formed project companies, such as major infrastructure projects, which can have shorter trading records
• Must be under substantially the same management for the two preceding full financial years and up until the date of listing
• Must have a continuity of ownership and control for the preceding full financial year and up until the date of listing

### Minimum public float
• Market capitalisation of at least HK$45 million held by the public at the time of listing
• Minimum 25% of the issuers’ total issued share capital at the time of listing
• The Exchange may, at its discretion, accept a percentage of public float between 15% and 25% if the market capitalisation of the issuer at the time of listing exceeds HK$10 billion

### Future prospects
• Statement of business objectives for the period covering the remaining financial year during which listing occurs and the two full financial years thereafter
• Inclusion of profit forecast is optional

### Accountants’ report
• Must be prepared in accordance with Hong Kong Financial Reporting Standards, International Financial Reporting Standards or the Accounting Standards for Business Enterprises of the People’s Republic of China (PRC CAS)
• Generally accepted accounting principles in the United States of America (US GAAP) are acceptable if the company is listed, or will be simultaneously listed, on either the New York Stock Exchange or the NASDAQ National Market
• In general, accountants’ report covers at least two complete financial years, immediately preceding the issue of the prospectus
• The latest financial period reported on by the reporting accountants must not have ended more than six months before the date of the listing document

### Corporate governance
• At least three independent non-executive directors required
• Must appoint independent non-executive directors representing at least one-third of the board
• Audit committee required
• Remuneration committee required
• Compliance officer required
• Required to appoint a compliance adviser for the period commencing on initial listing and ending on publication of financial results for the two full financial year after listing

### Acceptable jurisdictions
• Austria, Australia, Bermuda, Brazil, British Virgin Islands, Canada — Alberta, Canada — British Columbia, Canada — Ontario, Cayman Islands, Cyprus, England and Wales, France, Germany, Guernsey, Hong Kong, India, Ireland, Israel, Italy, Japan, Jersey, Labuan, Luxembourg, Netherlands, Russia, Singapore, Republic of Korea, The Isle of Man, The People’s Republic of China, United States of America — The State of California, United States of America — The State of Delaware, United States of America — The State of Nevada
• Other jurisdictions which are subject to appropriate standards of shareholder protection equivalent to those required under Hong Kong law will be considered
• Other jurisdictions for secondary listing may be considered

### Restrictions on controlling shareholders
• A controlling shareholder at the time of listing must undertake:
- Not to dispose of his interest in the issuer from the prospectus issue date to the first 12 months after listing
- Not to dispose of his interest in the issuer in the next 12 months such that he would cease to be a controlling shareholder: at least 30% interest in the issuer

### Other considerations
• Competing businesses of controlling shareholders, substantial shareholders or directors are allowed but full disclosure is required
• Mandatory public offering of at least 10% of the total offer size
• No underwriting requirement
• Listed issuer is not allowed to issue new securities in the first six months after listing except for the purpose of acquiring assets which will complement its business



# 14 Regulatory Environment

## 14.1 Major Regulatory Authorities Roles, Responsibilities and Relationships

### 14.1.1 Hong Kong Institute of Certified Public Accountants (HKICPA)

### 14.1.2 Securities and Futures Commission (SFC)

### 14.1.3 Financial Reporting Council (FRC)

### 14.1.4 Hong Kong Monetary Authority (HKMA)

### 14.1.5 Insurance Authority (IA)

### 14.1.6 Hong Kong Exchanges and Clearing Limited (HKEx)

### 14.1.7 Companies Ordinance (CO)

# 14.2 Key Codes of Conduct and Regulations
As described in Section 14.1, the major regulatory authorities authorised by the SFO include SFC, HKEx, Hong Kong stock and futures exchanges and the clearing houses. The SFO recognises the SFC as the primary regulator of the securities and futures markets and the HKEx as the controller of the only stock and futures exchange in Hong Kong. The HKEx operates through three subsidiaries: the Stock Exchange of Hong Kong, the Hong Kong Futures Exchange and the clearing houses.

This section outlines the regulatory framework and provides the key features of the various codes of conduct and regulation.

## 14.2.1 Securities and Futures Ordinance Code of Conduct
The SFC developed and published the Securities and Futures Ordinance code of conduct (SFO Code) based on the General Principles listed below. The SFO Code provides guidance on the interpretation, application, scope and effects of breaches of the code.

The SFO Code describes the General Principles (see https://www.sfc.hk/web/EN/assets/ components/codes/files-previous/web/codes/code-of-conduct-for-persons-licensed-by-orregistered-with-the-securities-and-futures-commission/2017-06-08%20-%202016-03-23%20 -%20Code%20of%20Conduct%20for%20Persons%20Licensed%20by%20or%20Registered%20with%20the%20Securities%20and%20Futures%20Commission.pdf) as follows:

• Honesty and fairness – In conducting its business activities, a licensed or registered person should act honestly, fairly and in the best interests of its clients and the integrity of the market.

• Diligence – In conducting its business activities, a licensed or registered person should act with due skill, care and diligence, in the best interests of its clients and the integrity of the market.

• Capabilities – A licensed or registered person should have and employ effectively the resources and procedures that are needed for the proper performance of its business activities.

• Information about clients – A licensed or registered person should seek from its clients information about their financial situation, investment experience and investment objectives relevant to the services to be provided.

• Information for clients – A licensed or registered person should make adequate disclosure of relevant material information in its dealings with its clients.

• Conflicts of interest – A licensed or registered person should try to avoid conflicts of interest, and when they cannot be avoided, should ensure that its clients are fairly treated.

• Compliance – A licensed or registered person should comply with all regulatory requirements applicable to the conduct of its business activities so as to promote the best interests of clients and the integrity of the market.

• Client assets – A licensed or registered person should ensure that client assets are promptly and properly accounted for and adequately safeguarded.

• Responsibility of senior management – The senior management of a licensed or registered person should bear primary responsibility for ensuring the maintenance of appropriate standards of conduct and adherence to proper procedures by the firm.

The sections below provide a summary of the key components and content of the SFO Code.

### Interpretation and Application
The SFO Code applies to all registered and licensed professionals performing regulated activities. However, the SFO Commission may take circumstances into consideration while applying the Code, such as an individual’s position, their supervisory responsibilities or the level of control they hold over the situation. Breaches of the Code alone do not create judicial liabilities, but SFO proceeding or hearings are admissible evidence in court and the SFO Commission may determine that the professional does not possess adequate fitness or properness.

### Honesty and Fairness
The SFO Code requires professionals to use accurate and not misleading information for client’s representations and advertising. Professionals should also charge fair and reasonable fees for their services and should be familiar with the anti-bribery guidelines.

### Diligence
The SFO Code provides rules and guidance on execution, allocation, advice to clients, withholding orders, collection, separate accounts, position and reporting limits, order recording, best interest of clients and gifts by distributors.

### Capabilities
The SFO Code provides rules and guidance on staff and supervision and internal controls. Any person employed by a licensed or registered participant should be qualified to act in the assigned capacity and conduct business that is fit and proper. Staff should be supervised diligently and operations should be proceduralised with adequate controls to protect from misconduct.

### Information about Clients
Know-Your-Customer (KYC) rules and other client identity instructions are important rules to operate in the best interest of the public. Professionals and companies should undertake adequate efforts to confirm the identities of their clients and their investment profile.

### Client Agreement
Requirements of client agreements and minimum content are included in the SFO Code, including that agreements do not circumvent legal requirements.

### Discretionary Accounts

Transactions should be approved or authorised by the client unless written authority is granted by the client for agents to act at their discretion. Authorisation of discretionary accounts should be revisited at least annually and the necessary disclosures should be made available.

### Information for Clients
The SFO Code requires that licensed or registered professionals provide clients with information about the firm, prompt confirmations, adequate disclosures, financials on the firm and any corporate actions.

### Client Priority
Orders should be handled fairly and in the order in which they are received, and orders of the client should have priority over orders for their own account.

Procedures should be in place to ensure that employees do not deal on the basis of material non-public information (insider trading).

### Conflicts of Interest
The SFO Code indicates that professionals can advise or participate in a transaction in which the professional has a material interest, if the appropriate disclosures and other reasonable steps are taken to ensure fair treatment of the client.

### Client Assets
All client assets should be accounted for properly and promptly, and adequately safe-guarded. For assets held overseas, additional risk disclosures should be provided to the client.

### Compliance
The SFO Code requires that professionals comply with the law, rules, regulations and codes administered or issued by the SFO Commission and implements and maintains measures appropriate to ensuring compliance.

### Rebates, Soft Dollars, Connected Transactions
Clients may pay through a broker (soft dollar) if the goods or services provided are of demonstrable benefit and within execution and price standards, if the client has consented in writing and if the transaction is properly disclosed.

Client transaction rebates can be retained only if the client has consented in writing to retaining the rebates, the brokerage rates are not excessive and the rebates are appropriately disclosed.

If a client exercises the right to cancel an order, sell the product back or otherwise unwind the transaction, it should be executed promptly and the client should be refunded the full amount less a reasonable administrative charge.

### Responsibility of Senior Management
Senior management should manage risks associated with the business. Senior management should understand the nature of the business, its internal control procedures and its policies on the assumption of risk. To that end they should have access to all relevant information and advice available to them.

### Professional Investors
The SFO provides detailed definitions, restrictions and exceptions for the following three types:

1. Institutional professional investor
2. Corporate professional investor
3. Individual professional investor

### Analysts
An analyst is any individual within a firm who prepares and/or publishes investment research or the substance of investment research, although it doesn’t apply to someone who gives advice incidentally to his function, it is internal and not for external distribution or is personal advice. The SFO defines associate, financial interest, firm, individual employed by or associated with the firm, investment research, issuer, new listing applicant, prospectus, REIT and securities.

The SFO Code provides principles for analysts in ss.16.1–16.11, specifically:

• Analyst trading and financial interests: Mechanisms should exist so that analysts’ trading activities or financial interests do not prejudice their investment research and recommendations.

• Firm financial interests and business relationships: Mechanisms should exist so that analysts’ investment research and recommendations are not prejudiced by the trading activities, financial interests or business relationships of the firms that employ them.

• Analyst reporting lines and compensation: Reporting lines for analysts and their compensation arrangements should be structured to eliminate or severely limit actual and potential conflicts of interest.

• Firm compliance systems: Firms that employ analysts should establish written internal procedures or controls to identify and eliminate, avoid, manage or disclose actual and potential analyst conflicts of interest.

• Outside influence: The undue influence of securities issuers, institutional investors and other outside parties upon analysts should be eliminated or managed.

• Clarity, specificity and prominence of disclosure: Disclosures of actual and potential conflicts of interest should be complete, timely, clear, concise, specific and prominent.

• Integrity and ethical behaviour: Analysts should be held to high integrity standards.

### Sponsors
A sponsor’s primary role is to provide assurance to the market that the listing applicant complies with the Listing Rules and other relevant legal and regulatory requirements and that the listing document provides sufficient particulars and information for investors to form a valid and justifiable opinion of the listing applicant’s shares, financial condition and profitability.

A sponsor should comply with the following key requirements in order to discharge its role satisfactorily. The following requirements are defined in the Sponsors section of the SFO Code (ss.17.1–17.15):

• Advising a listing applicant: A sponsor should advise and guide a listing applicant in preparation for a listing.

• Reasonable due diligence: A sponsor should take reasonable due diligence steps in respect of a listing application; before submitting a listing application a sponsor should complete all reasonable due diligence on a listing applicant except in relation to matters that by their nature can only be dealt with at a later date.

• Disclosure to the market: A sponsor should take reasonable steps to ensure that true, accurate and complete disclosure about a listing applicant is made to the public.

• Communication with the regulators: A sponsor should deal with the regulators in a truthful, cooperative and prompt manner.

• Proper records: A sponsor should maintain proper books and records that are sufficient to demonstrate its compliance with the Code.

• Resources, systems and controls: A sponsor should maintain sufficient resources and effective systems and controls for proper implementation and adequate management oversight of the sponsor work.

• Overall management of a public offer: A sponsor should act as the overall manager of a public offer to ensure that the public offer is conducted in a fair and orderly manner.

• Information provided to analysts in new listings: A sponsor should take reasonable steps to ensure analysts do not receive material information not disclosed in the listing document.

### Electronic Trading
The SFO Code defines the following terms in s.18.2

• ‘Algorithmic trading’ means computer generated trading activities created by a predetermined set of rules aimed at delivering specific execution outcomes.

• ‘Algorithmic trading system’ means the system through which algorithmic trading is conducted. It includes a system designed and developed in house or by a third party service provider.

• ‘Direct market access’ means the access to a market provided to a client by a licensed or registered person through which the client transmits orders, directly or indirectly, to the market’s trade matching system for execution under the licensed or registered person’s identifier other than those initiated by way of internet trading.

• ‘Electronic trading’ means the trading of securities and futures contracts electronically and includes internet trading, direct market access and algorithmic trading.

• ‘Electronic trading system’ means the system through which electronic trading is conducted. It includes a system designed and developed in-house or by a third party service provider.

• ‘Internet trading’ means an arrangement where order instructions are sent to a licensed or registered person through its internet-based trading facility. An internetbased trading facility may be accessed through a computer, mobile device or other electronic device.

### Responsibilities of Licensed/Registered Persons
• Orders: the settlement and financial obligations of orders sent to the market through its electronic trading system, management and supervision.

• Management and supervision: manage and adequately supervise the design, development, deployment and operation of the electronic trading system.

• Adequacy of system: ensure the integrity of the electronic trading system it uses or provides to clients for use, as may be appropriate in the circumstances, including the system’s reliability, security and capacity, and have appropriate contingency measures in place.

• Record-keeping: keep proper records on the design, development, deployment and operation of its electronic trading system.

• Risk management: ensure that all the client orders are transmitted to the infrastructure used by the licensed or registered person and are subject to: (a) appropriate automated pre-trade risk management controls; and (b) regular post-trade monitoring.

### Alternative Liquidity Pools (ALP)
These rules apply to those that operate an alternative liquidity pool or routes client orders to an alternative liquidity pool for execution.

SFO Code defines the following:

• ALP means an electronic system operated by a licensed or registered person through which the crossing/matching of orders involving listed or exchange traded securities is conducted with no pre-trade transparency.

• ‘Authorised trader’ means an individual who is authorised by a user to place orders into an ALP. For the avoidance of doubt, a licensed or registered person operating an ALP is not an authorised trader in relation to orders placed into its ALP.

• Proprietary order is defined in s.19.2.e of the SFO Code as an order which is for:
- The account of a licensed or registered person operating an ALP, trading as principal;
- The account of any user, which is a company within the same group of companies as the licensed or registered person operating an ALP, trading as principal;
- Any account in which a licensed or registered person operating an ALP, or any user which is a company within the same group of companies as the licensed or registered person, has an interest; or
- The account of any employee or agent of a licensed or registered person operating an ALP or of any user which is a company within the same group of companies as the licensed or registered person.

The SFO Code includes the following rules and guidance related to ALMs ss.19.3–19.12:

• Management and supervision: A licensed or registered person operating an ALP should effectively manage and adequately supervise the design, development, deployment and operation of its ALP, as may be appropriate in the circumstances.

• Access to ALPs:
- All licensed or registered persons should establish and implement measures to ensure that only qualified investors are permitted to be users of an ALP.
- A licensed or registered person should only route orders to an ALP on behalf of clients where such orders are placed or originated by a person who is a qualified investor.

• Operation of ALPs: A licensed or registered person operating an ALP may allow transactions to be placed into, and transacted in, its ALP at such times as it considers appropriate.

• Order priority: Irrespective of the time when orders are placed, a licensed or registered person operating an ALP should ensure that the orders of users which are not proprietary orders have priority over proprietary orders when such orders are being transacted at the same price.

• Information for users:
°A licensed or registered person operating an ALP should, by means of ALP Guidelines, provide sufficiently comprehensive information to the users of the ALP to ensure that they are fully informed as to the manner in which the ALP operates.
°Prior to routing any order to an ALP on behalf of a client for the first time, a licensed or registered person should ensure that the ALP Guidelines have been brought to the attention of the person placing or originating the order.

• Opting out of ALP: A licensed or registered person operating an ALP should permit the users to opt out of matching or crossing their orders in its ALP.

• Adequacy of system: A licensed or registered person operating an ALP should ensure the integrity of the ALP as may be appropriate in the circumstances, including the controls, reliability, security and capacity of the ALP, and have appropriate contingency measures in place in case of any failure.

• Record keeping: A licensed or registered person operating an ALP should keep, or cause to be kept, proper records concerning the design, development, deployment and operation of its ALP.

• Risk management: A licensed or registered person operating an ALP should have controls that are reasonably designed to ensure:
°The integrity of its trading methodology; and
°That its trading methodology operates in the interest of preserving the integrity of the market.

• Reporting and notification obligations:
°A licensed or registered person operating an ALP should have procedures in place to ensure that information concerning transactions conducted on its ALP is appropriately reported or made available to its users, exchanges, the Commission and other regulators.
°A licensed or registered person operating an ALP should keep the Commission informed of any change in relation to the operation of its ALP and any breach arising out of its operation.

#### Illustrative Example 18

As AB Co Ltd expands into different geographic areas in Hong Kong, the management of the bank actively seeks out strategies for obtaining new clients in their current and new locations. During the strategy discussions, the head of the compliance department recommends certain enhancements to the customer due diligence program to be consistent with the regulatory expectations.

The head of the compliance department recommends a formal Know-Your-Customer (KYC) program to fully vet prospective clients, continually monitor current clients and ensure products offered to them are understood and not overly complex. He recommends the following program outline:

1. Verify the identity of the customer through the collection of information regarding beneficial ownership, incorporation/partnership agreements, location, business activities and third-party confirmations. This is to be performed for all customers.

2. Determine if the customer is listed as a known criminal through reference of regulatory and government databases.

3. Assess the risk of each client through a search for red flags by considering the nature of the business, expected transaction patterns, payment methods and origin, anti-money laundering policies, third-party documentation and reputation. Customers determined to be at higher risk require senior management approval.

4. Monitor the clients and their activity through implementing reporting protocols for transaction thresholds or patterns and requiring periodic re-performance of the full due diligence.

These KYC programs would ensure that AB Co Ltd performs the appropriate due diligence on their customers to verify their identities and understand their investment profile. Through the verification of customer identity, the bank can mitigate the risk of noncompliance with the anti-money laundering and counter financing terrorism (AML/CFT) rules and other relevant regulations. Gaining a complete understanding of client’s investment profiles allows the bank to ensure the clients understand the products and that they are not overly complex.

## 14.2.2 Corporate Finance Advisor Code of Conduct
The SFC developed and published the Corporate Finance Adviser Code of Conduct (CFA Code) to set out the requirements and guidelines in respect to the conduct of corporate finance advisers (CFAs). The CFA Code defines a CFA as ‘Persons or entities who carry on the business of advising on corporate finance in Hong Kong and are licensed or registered under the Securities and Futures Ordinance (Cap.571) as a licensed representative, licensed corporation or registered institution’. The CFA Code details the purpose of the code, defines corporate finance advice, the status of the code, as well as codifies the requirements and guidelines.

The following sections summarise the requirements and guidelines of the CFA Code.

### Conduct of Business
The CFA Code outlines the professional requirements related to licensing, management of the business, books and records, staff supervision, compliance and training.

### Competence
CFAs should act with competence in regard to their integrity, demonstration of competence and professional advice.

### Conflicts of Interest
The general requirement of the CFA Code regarding conflicts of interest is that CFAs should ‘(a) take all reasonable steps to avoid situations that are likely to involve a conflict of interest; (b) not unfairly place its interests above those of its clients; and (c) withdraw from, or decline to accept, a mandate where a material conflict of interest arises with its client that cannot be resolved through its client giving its informed consent’.

### Standard of Work
CFAs should deliver the highest standard of work at all times. The CFA Code provides guidelines for a specific area of CFA work, including:

• Due skill and care.
• Engagement letter.
• Reliance on work by experts or other professionals.
• Financial adviser to a listing application.
• Reliance on information from the client.
• Avoid undue delay.
• Standard of documents.
• Use of plain language.

### Duties to the Client
CFAs should act in the best interest of clients at all times. The CFA Code requires a comprehensive KYC program, the safeguarding of confidential information, advice to clients regarding their behaviour and the CFA’s conduct towards their clients.

### Communication with Regulators
The CFA Code requires CFAs to openly cooperate with regulators and to consult regulators early when an issue or potential issue is identified. Dealing with the Regulators should be done by the appropriate staff.

### Personal Account Dealings

The CFA Code requires that personal account dealings are conducted properly and that CFA have policy and procedures to guide, prohibit and monitor personal account dealings.

#### Illustrative Example 19

A local financial advisory firm in Hong Kong has implemented certain processes to ensure the firm’s advisory services meet high standards by clients and the regulators.

The firm has a rigorous recruiting programme to hire highly skilled and competent employees. It also requires minimum training to be completed each year and employees to earn the appropriate credentials to work in certain functions or at certain levels. There are also multiple layers of review and approval for all client deliverables. Where necessary, the firm outsources specialised analysis to third-party experts.

It has constructed templates for most client deliverables to ensure consistent quality and clarity. The templates standardise communications to the client and use plain language to ensure the information is optimally understood.

## 14.2.3 Listing Rules and Stock Exchange Regulation for Listed Companies
This section explains the rules and regulation on exchange listing by the Hong Kong Exchanges and Clearing Market (HKEX).

### 14.2.3.1 Main Board
The Main Board rules apply to all listings on HKEx other than the GEM (formerly known as Growth Enterprise Market) and do not apply to options contracts traded in the options system. The rules derive their authority from the Securities and Futures Ordinance with the overall objective of providing ‘a fair, orderly and efficient market for trading of securities’. The listing rules include requirements to be met prior to listing as well as after listing.

According to the General Principles outlined in the Introduction Section of the `Main Board Listing Rules` (see https://en-rules.hkex.com.hk/sites/default/files/net_file_store/new_rulebooks/ c/o/consol_mb.pdf), the rules generally aim to accomplish the following:

• Applications are suitable for listing.
• The issue and marketing of securities is conducted in a fair and orderly manner and that potential investors are given sufficient information to enable them to make a properly informed assessment of an issuer and, in the case of a guaranteed issue, the guarantor and of the securities for which listing is sought.
• Investors and the public are kept fully informed by listed issuers and, in the case of a guaranteed issue, the guarantors of material factors which might affect their interests.
• All holders of listed securities are treated fairly and equally.
• Directors of a listed issuer act in the interests of its shareholders as a whole particularly where the public represents only a minority of the shareholders.
• All new issues of equity securities by a listed issuer are first offered to the existing shareholders by way of rights unless they have agreed otherwise.

The minimum requirements for listing on the Main Board (see https://www.hkex.com.hk/-/ media/HKEX-Market/Listing/Getting-Started/201802/201802-new-Listing_en.pdf) include the following:

• Satisfaction of one of the following:
°Three-year aggregate profit of not less than HK$50 million (not less than HK$20 million in the latest year and not less than HK$30 million in the two preceding years) and market capitalisation of at least HK$500 million;
°Latest year revenue of at least HK$500 million and market capitalisation of at least HK$4 billion; or
°Latest year revenue of at least HK$500 million, market capitalisation of at least HK$2 billion and three-year aggregate operating cash flow of at least HK$100 million.

• 25% minimum public float value, which represents the value of shares held by public investors (can reduce to 15% if market capitalisation is greater than HK$10 billion).
• Minimum of 300 shareholders.
• Three years of management continuity and one year of ownership continuity.
• At least three independent directors that control at least one third of the board.
• Semi-annual financial reporting.

While the sections below introduce the main components of the Main Board rules, professionals should diligently review the entire rule-set to gain familiarity with the structure and content of the rules. Additionally, refer to Chapters 9 and 12 of Module 10 for details on the listing rules and requirements as well as their implication and application in business.

The general section of the Main Board rules covers the application procedures for listing on HKEx, including rules for new applicants, cancellation, disciplinary measures and rights of the participants and the Listing Committee. The Listing Committee is an independent review body that maintains the right to review any decision and imposes regulations or restrictions. The general section also provides rules and guidance on governance on issuers such as authorised representatives, directors, board committees, the company secretary and sponsors. Further accountant reports are subject to the Main Board rules as they include specific requirements on types of reports, content and format of reports, modifications and disclosures.

The equity securities section provides rules and guidance on the listing of equity securities. Specifically, the code describes the methods and qualifications for applying for and listing of equity securities in various circumstances. The rules also cover voting power and voting rights to increase investor protection, the process for transferring a listed equity security from the GEM to Main Board, documentation, prospectus and publication requirements, as well as other related topics.

The Main Board rules apply to issues of equity securities or debt securities by investment companies, and the rules outline additional requirements for listing of securities issued by investment companies, unit trusts, mutual funds or any other collective investment scheme.

The Main Board rules describe the methods of listing debt securities, including the offer for subscription, offer for sale, placing, exchange or conversions, and other methods. The rules detail the qualifications and the application procedures, the documentation requirements and the listing agreement requirements. Clarification and additional guidance are provided for options, warrants and convertible debt securities.

#### Listing Regime for Companies from Emerging and Innovative Sectors
In April 2018 the HKEx implemented new rules guiding the listing process for companies in emerging and innovative sectors. According to a HKEx press-release (see https://www.hkex. com.hk/News/News-Release/2018/180424news), the following three new chapters were added to the Main Board Listing Rules:

1. Biotech Company Chapter: ‘Permit listings of biotech issuers that do not meet any of the Main Board financial eligibility tests’.

	The new chapter includes the following additional listing requirements:
	• At least HK$1.5 billion market capitalisation.
	• In current line of business with the same management for at least two years.
	• At least 125% working capital for a minimum 12 months.

2. WVR Company Chapter: ‘Permit listings of companies with weighted voting right (WVR) structures’.
	The new chapter outlines listing and suitability requirements for both companies and WVT beneficiaries:

	• Companies:
	°Greater than or equal to HK$10 billion market capitalisation with at least HK$1 billion in revenue if market capitalisation is less than HK$40 billion;
	°Meet the definition of ‘innovative’ per guidelines;
	°Successful track record of high growth; and
	°‘Meaningful’ third party investment for external validation.

	• WVR Beneficiaries:
	°Materially responsible for growth of the business; and
	°Hold between 10% and 50% of the shares collectively at listing.

3. Secondary Listing Chapter: ‘Establish a new concessionary secondary listing route for Greater China and international companies that wish to have a secondary list in Hong Kong’.

	This chapter establishes the new qualifying criteria for a new secondary listing:
	• Listed on a qualifying exchange (New York Stock Exchange (NYSE), NASDAQ or London Stock Exchange (LSE)’s Main Market).
	• Two years good compliance record on qualifying exchange.
	• Greater than or equal to HK$10 billion market capitalisation with at least HK$1 billion in revenue if market capitalisation is less than HK$40 billion.

The HKEx recognises the potential risks of listing companies that have not yet earned revenue and has implemented certain safeguards to mitigate those risks. The safeguards include additional detailed criteria for determining suitability, higher market capitalisation and additional disclosure requirements.

### 14.2.3.2 GEM
The GEM is the exchange on which growth companies that do not meet the Main Board requirements are listed. While the GEM is a part of the HKEX and the Securities and Futures Ordinance, it is a distinct entity from the Main Board, therefore the Main Board rules do not apply to the GEM and any company that seeks to move on to the Main Board must re-apply and meet the Main Board requirements.

The GEM rules, like the Main Board rules, do not apply to options contracts made through the options system.

According to the General Principles outlined in the Introduction Section of the GEM listing rules (https://en-rules.hkex.com.hk/sites/default/files/net_file_store/new_rulebooks/c/o/ consol_gem.pdf), the rules generally aim to accomplish the following, which are the same as the principles of the Main Board Listing Rules:
• Applications are suitable for listing.
• The issue and marketing of securities is conducted in a fair and orderly manner and potential investors are given sufficient information to enable them to make a properly informed assessment of an issuer and, in the case of a guaranteed issue, the guarantor and of the securities for which listing is sought.
• Investors and the public are kept fully informed by listed issuers and, in the case of a guaranteed issue, the guarantors of material factors which might affect their interests.
• All holders of listed securities are treated fairly and equally.
• Directors of a listed issuer act in the interests of its shareholders as a whole particularly where the public represents only a minority of the shareholders.
• All new issues of equity securities by a listed issuer are first offered to the existing shareholders by way of rights unless they have agreed otherwise.

The minimum requirements for listing on the GEM (see https://www.hkex.com.hk/-/media/ HKEX-Market/Listing/Getting-Started/201802/201802-new-Listing_en.pdf) include the following:
• Two-year aggregate operating cash flow of at least HK$30 million.
• Market capitalisation of at least HK$150 million.
• 25% minimum public float value, which represents the value of shares held by public investors (can reduce to 15% if market capitalisation is greater than HK$10 billion).
• Minimum of 100 shareholders.
• Two years of management continuity and one year of ownership continuity.
• At least three independent directors that control at least one third of the board.
• Quarterly financial reporting.

The GEM rules also provide the detail requirements for the specific areas including the application procedures for both new applicants and listing issuers, the cancellation and disciplinary procedures and the GEM Listing Committee.

Refer to Chapter 12 of Module 10 for details of the listing rules and requirements as well as their implication and application in business.

#### Apply and Analyse 2

XYZ Limited, an office supply retailer based in Hong Kong, has been in operation for over two decades and has maintained steady growth. The company has a current market capitalisation of HK$550 million. It files quarterly audited financial reports and it has earned profits of HK$22 million in the latest year and HK$37 million in the two preceding years. Last year the company’s revenue was HK$387 million. Ownership of the company has not changed in the past ten years. Shares are held by 415 shareholders, of which approximately 45% are public investors. The board of directors is comprised of 12 directors, four of which are independent.

XYZ Limited is now considering issuing an IPO to go public. It has hired your advisory firm to advise whether it meets the minimum requirements to be listed on the HKEx Main Board. It would like to determine whether they should invest the time and resources to formally apply for listing.

Analysis

You provide the following analysis to the management of XYZ Limited:

Based on our review of XYZ Limited, we have reached the conclusion that XYZ Limited satisfies the minimum requirements for listing on the Main Board.

XYZ Limited meets the financial performance requirements with a three-year aggregate profit of HK$59 million and a market capitalisation of HK$550 million. Regarding the requirements on ownership structure and continuity, XYZ Limited satisfies the requirements with 415 investors, approximately 45% of which are owned by public investors and the ownership has not changed in the past ten years. Listing on the Main Board also requires at least three independent directors controlling at least one third of the board of directors and XYZ Limited meets these requirements with four independent directors out of 12 total directors. Additionally, the listing requires semi-annual financial reporting and XYZ Limited files quarterly audited financial reports.
---- 
For both the Main Board and GEM, the HKEx also requires specific Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR) reporting and disclosures to be made on an annual basis. The CSR reporting takes the form of an Environmental, Social, and Corporate Governance (ESG) Report and the HKEx rules encourage this report to be a part of the issuer’s annual report. Certain ESG disclosures will become mandatory, with other disclosures elevated from ‘recommended’ to being required on a ‘comply or explain’ basis. There will no longer be any recommended disclosures as the current recommended KPI disclosures on social aspects to ESG reporting, such as employment and labour practices, will be elevated to ‘comply or explain’ disclosures. The HKEx CSR reporting guidance published by the HKEx provides the following Reporting Principles to guide the preparation of the ESG Report, including the content and format of the reports. The four reporting principles are **materiality, quantitative, balance and consistency**.

The HKEx also requires a discussion of ESG in the Business Review Section of the Director’s Report. This discussion is intended to complement the ESG Report and the Corporate Governance Code. The Business Review Section of the Director’s Report must include the following:
• A discussion of the issuer’s environmental policies and performance;
• A discussion of the issuer’s compliance with the relevant laws and regulations that have a significant impact on the issuer; and
• An account of the issuer’s key relationships with its employees, customers and suppliers and others that have a significant impact on the issuer and on which the issuer’s success depends.

Effective for financial years beginning in July 2020 or later, the HKEx has introduced several amendments to the Listing Rules related to CSR Reporting. Below is a summary of the changes.
• Introducing mandatory disclosure requirements to include:
°A board statement setting out the board’s consideration of ESG issues and its ESG management approach and strategy (including the process used to evaluate, prioritise and manage material ESG issues and the business risks), together with an explanation on how the board reviews progress against its ESG-related goals and targets and how they relate to its business;
°A description or explanation on how the reporting principles (materiality, quantitative and consistency) are applied in preparing the ESG report;
°An explanation of reporting boundaries of ESG reports and the process used to identify entities included in the report;
• Requiring disclosure of significant climate-related issues which have impacted and may impact the issuer;
• Amending the ‘Environmental’ key performance indicators (KPIs) to require disclosure of relevant targets;
• Upgrading the disclosure obligation of all ‘Social’ KPIs to ‘comply or explain’; and
• Shortening the deadline for publication of ESG reports to within five months after the financial year-end.

## 14.2.4 Regulations on Takeovers and Mergers and Share Buy back
The Codes on Takeovers and Mergers and Share Buy backs (Takeover Code) is maintained by the SFC and is a voluntary code with the overall objective to ensure fair and equal treatment of all shareholders who are affected by takeovers, mergers and share buy backs.

The Takeover Code does not have the rule of law but mandates timely and adequate disclosures and provides a framework for which takeovers, mergers and share buy backs are to be performed. The Takeover Code applies to takeovers, mergers and share buy backs that affect public companies in Hong Kong.

General principles of the Takeover Code (see https://www.sfc.hk/web/EN/assets/ components/codes/files-current/web/codes/the-codes-on-takeovers-and-mergers-and-sharebuy-backs/the-codes-on-takeovers-and-mergers-and-share-buy-backs.pdf) are defined in the General Principles section:

• All shareholders are to be treated even-handedly and all shareholders of the same class are to be treated similarly.

• If control of a company changes or is acquired or is consolidated, a general offer to all other shareholders is normally required. Where an acquisition is contemplated as a result of which a person may incur such an obligation, he must, before making the acquisition, ensure that he can and will continue to be able to implement such an offer.

• During the course of an offer, or when an offer is in contemplation, neither an offeror, nor the offeree company, nor any of their respective advisers may furnish information to some shareholders that is not made available to all shareholders. This principle does not apply to the furnishing of information in confidence by the offeree company to a bona fide potential offeror or vice versa.

• An offeror should announce an offer only after careful and responsible consideration.

The same applies to making acquisitions that may lead to an obligation to make a general offer. In either case the offeror and its financial advisers should be satisfied that it can and will continue to be able to implement the offer in full.

• Shareholders should be given sufficient information, advice and time to reach an informed decision on an offer. No relevant information should be withheld. All documents must, as in the case with a prospectus, be prepared with the highest possible degree of care, responsibility and accuracy.

• All persons concerned with offers should make full and prompt disclosure of all relevant information and take every precaution to avoid the creation or continuance of a false market. Parties involved in offers must take care that statements are not made that may mislead shareholders or the market.

• Rights of control should be exercised in good faith and the oppression of minority or non-controlling shareholders is always unacceptable.

• Directors of an offeror and the offeree company must always, in advising their shareholders, act only in their capacity as directors and not have regard to their personal or family shareholdings or to their personal relationships with the companies. They should only consider the shareholders’ interests taken as a whole when they are giving advice to shareholders. Directors of the offeree company should give careful consideration before they enter into any commitment with an offeror (or anyone else) that would restrict their freedom to advise their shareholders. Such commitments may give rise to conflicts of interest or result in a breach of the directors’ fiduciary duties.

• At no time after a bona fide offer has been communicated to the board of the offeree company, or after the board of the offeree company has reason to believe that a bona fide offer might be imminent, may the board of the offeree company take any action in relation to the affairs of the company, without the approval of shareholders in general meeting, which could effectively result in any bona fide offer being frustrated or in the shareholders being denied an opportunity to decide on its merits.

• All parties concerned with transactions subject to the Codes are required to co-operate to the fullest extent with the Executive Director of the Corporate Finance Division of the Commission, the Panel and the Takeovers Appeal Committee, and to provide all relevant information.

In 2019, to address the misuse of shell companies to maintain listed status, the rules on reverse takeovers and continuing list criteria were amended to ensure that listed issuers maintain sufficient operations and asset values to warrant continued listing. The following summarises the key listing rule amendments and the criteria for continuing listing.

• The definition of reverse takeovers (RTOs) is amended along with modifications to existing rules:

°The RTO principle-based test codifies six assessment factors with modifications to changes in control and transactions such as acquisitions and disposals. The six assessment factors are the relative size of acquisition, fundamental change in the issuer’s principal business, nature and scale of the issuer’s business, quality of acquisition targets, change in control or de facto control of the listed issuer, and change in de facto control arising from issue of restricted convertible securities.
°The RTO bright-line test restricts disposals by issuers of substantial business within 36 months of a change in control of the issuer.
°Disallowing backdoor listing through large-scale issue of securities for cash or a change in control where the proceeds would be used to acquire new business that is substantially larger than the issuer’s principal existing business.
°Extreme Very Substantial Acquisitions are renamed Extreme Transactions and additional eligibility criteria are imposed. The issuer must operate a principal business of substantial size, or the issuer must have been under the control or de facto control of the same person(s) for a long period (normally not less than 36 months) and the transaction will not result in a change in control or de facto control of the issuer.

• Three amendments to the continuing listing criteria for listed issuers:
°The rule on sufficient operations requires issuers to maintain a sufficient level of operations and have assets of sufficient value. Proprietary trading is excluded when performing this evaluation.
°The rule on cash companies extends the definition of short-dated securities to include those that are easily convertible into cash and rename it as ‘short-term investments’; and confine the exemption to cash and short-term investments held by members of an issuer’s group that are banking companies, insurance companies or securities houses.
°A twelve-month transitional period from October 1 2019 applies to listed issuers that do not comply with the amendments above on sufficient operations and cash companies.

## 14.2.5 Regulation on Issuance of Convertible Bonds, Rights Issues and Share Options
Regulations on the issuance of convertible bonds, rights and share options are detailed in the Main Board Listing Rules and the GEM Listing rules discussed in Sections 14.2.3.1 and 14.2.3.2. This section provides details on these rules. Note that there are only minor variations between the Main Board and GEM Listing Rules for the issuance of convertible bonds, rights and share options.

### 14.2.5.1 Convertible debt securities

Chapter 28 of the Main Board Listing Rules (see https://www.hkex.com.hk/-/media/HKEXMarket/Listing/Rules-and-Guidance/Listing-Rules-Contingency/Main-Board-ListingRules/Debt-Securities/chapter_28.pdf?la=en) and Chapter 34 of the GEM Listing Rules (see https://www.hkex.com.hk/-/media/HKEX-Market/Listing/Rules-and-Guidance/Listing-RulesContingency/GEM-Listing-Rules/Debt-Securities/chapter_34.pdf?la=en) list the following rules for convertible debt securities:

• All convertible debt securities must, prior to the issue thereof, be approved by HKEx and HKEx should be consulted at the earliest opportunity as to the requirements that will apply.

• All convertible debt securities that are convertible into new equity securities or outstanding securities of the issuer or a company in the same group as the issuer for which a listing is to be sought must comply both with the requirements applicable to the debt securities for which listing is sought and with the requirements applicable to the underlying equity securities to which such convertible debt securities relate. In the event of any conflict or inconsistency between the various requirements, those applicable to such equity securities shall prevail.

• Under the Main Board Listing Rules, convertible debt securities that are convertible into equity securities may be listed only if such equity securities are (or will become at the same time):
a. A class of listed equity securities; or
b. A class of equity securities listed or dealt with on another regulated, regularly operating, open stock market recognised by HKEx.

Under the GEM Listing Rules, convertible debt securities which are convertible into equity securities may be listed only if such equity securities are (or will become at the same time) a class of equity securities listed on GEM.

However, HKEx may list convertible debt securities in other circumstances if it is satisfied that holders have the necessary information available to form an opinion concerning the value of the underlying equity securities to which such convertible debt securities relate. Under the Main Board Listing Rules, this does not apply to an issue of convertible debt securities by a State or a Supranational.

• Convertible debt securities that are convertible into property other than equity securities may be listed only if HKex is satisfied that holders have the necessary information available to form an opinion concerning the value of the other property to which such convertible debt securities relate. Under the Main Board Listing Rules, this does not apply to an issue of convertible debt securities by a State or a supranational.

• Any alterations in the terms of convertible debt securities after issue must be approved by HKEx, except where the alterations take effect automatically under the existing terms of such convertible debt securities.

### 14.2.5.2 Options, warrants and similar rights

Chapter 27 of the Main Board Listing Rules (see https://www.hkex.com.hk/-/media/HKEXMarket/Listing/Rules-and-Guidance/Listing-Rules-Contingency/Main-Board-Listing-Rules/ Debt-Securities/chapter_27.pdf) and Chapter 33 of the GEM Listing Rules (See https:// en-rules.hkex.com.hk/sites/default/files/net_file_store/new_rulebooks/h/k/HKEX4476_1755_ VER10.pdf) present the following rules for options, warrants and similar rights:

1. Applies both to options, warrants and similar rights to subscribe or purchase debt securities (‘warrants’) that are issued or granted on their own by an issuer or any of its subsidiaries and to warrants that are attached to other debt securities. Warrants that are attached to other securities but that are non-detachable are convertible securities and are also subject to the provisions of chapters for convertible equity securities or convertible debt securities, as appropriate.

2. Where application is made for the listing of warrants, HKEx will normally apply the same requirements as would apply to the underlying securities to be subscribed or purchased. However, where such an application is contemplated, HKEx should be consulted at the earliest opportunity as to the requirements that will apply.

3. Under the Main Board Listing Rules, warrants may be listed only if the underlying securities to be subscribed or purchased are (or will become at the same time):
	a. A class of listed debt securities; or	
	b. A class of debt securities listed or dealt in on another regulated, regularly operating, open stock market recognised by HKEx.

	Under the GEM Listing Rules, warrants may be listed only if the underlying securities to be subscribed or purchased are (or will become at the same time) a class of debt securities listed on GEM.

	However, HKEx may list warrants in other circumstances if it is satisfied that holders have the necessary information available to form an opinion concerning the value of the underlying debt securities to which such warrants relate. Under the Main Board Listing Rules, this does not apply to an issue of options, warrants or similar rights by a State or a Supranational.

4. Any alterations in the terms of warrants after issue or grant must be approved by HKEx, except where the alterations take effect automatically under the terms of such warrants. In particular HKEx should be consulted at the earliest opportunity where the issuer proposes to alter the exercise period or the exercise price.

## 14.2.6 Corporate Governance Code of Conduct

The Corporate Governance Code and Corporate Governance Report (The Corporate Governance Code) sets out the requirements and guidance to ensure sound and effective internal controls are in place to protect the stakeholder’s interests.

The Corporate Governance Code is incorporated into the Main Board Listing Rules (Appendix 14) and the GEM Listing Rules (Appendix 15), and therefore the Code applies to issuers accordingly. The HKICPA has issued further guidance based on these rules to assist issuers in understanding and implementing the rules. The sections below summarise the additional guidance.

The Corporate Governance Code provides corporate governance principles at the provision and recommendation levels , where provisions are required to be met and recommendations are encouraged guidance based on best practices. The Corporate Governance Code principles and provisions are organised into the sections listed below.

### Directors

This section describes the rules and guidance for the Board’s, Chairman’s and Chief Executive’s appointments and removal, the nomination committee, responsibilities of directors and access to information.

### Remuneration of Directors and Senior Management and Board Evaluation

The single principle on remuneration is focused on disclosure of the directors’ remuneration policy and other remuneration related matters and the transparency and formality of the procedure for setting policy on executive directors’ remuneration and all directors’ remuneration packages. It adds that directors should not be involved in their own remuneration discussion.

### Accountability and Audit

Accountability and audit principles cover financial reporting, risk management and internal control and the Audit Committee.

### Delegation by the Board

Principles governing delegation by the Board relate to the management functions, the Board committees and the corporate governance functions.

### Communication with Shareholders

The Code states that the Board should be responsible for maintaining an on-going dialogue with shareholders and, in particular, use annual general meetings or other general meetings to communicate with them and encourage their participation.

### Company Secretary

The company secretary is responsible for advising the Board on governance matters.

The Code requires that issuers also prepare and submit a Corporate Governance Report with their summary financial reports. The following details are required to be disclosed in the Corporate Governance Report.

• Corporate governance practices
• Director’s securities transactions
• Board of directors
• Chairman and Chief Executive
• Board committees
• Auditor’s remunerations
• Company secretary
• Shareholder’s rights
• Investor relations
• Risk management and internal control
• Share interest of senior management
• Investor relations
• Management functions

### Apply and Analyse 3

AB Co Ltd has not updated the firmwide code of conduct in several years, even as the company has grown considerably, has entered new markets and reorganised its structure. Management has assigned to you the task of designing AB Co Ltd’s new corporate code of conduct that reflects the activities of the firm and the regulatory expectations.

#### Analysis DRAFT FRAMEWORK:

AB Co Ltd Corporate Code of Conduct For review and comment by the Head of the Corporate Functions Department, AB Co Ltd.

Overview: AB Co Ltd expects employees to demonstrate behaviour in accordance with this code of conduct and other applicable regulatory and professional guidance to meet the firm’s business and social objectives. The code provides a conceptual framework based on the firm’s ethical principles as well as codified rules.

Conceptual Framework for Ethical Conduct Employees should perform their duties at the highest level of:
• Integrity, honesty and fairness, and act in the best interest of the client.
• Standard of work, competence, diligence, due skill and effective employment of resources, including the reliance of work by experts, use of plain language and avoidance of undue delay.

#### Code of Conduct Requirements

1. Conflicts of interest • Avoid situations that are likely to involve a conflict of interest.
	• Place client interests above their own.
	• Withdraw from, or decline to accept, a mandate where a material conflict of interest arises with its client that cannot be resolved through its client giving its informed consent.
	• Ensure that its clients are fairly treated.

2. Duties to the client
	• Act in the best interest of clients at all times.
	• Comprehensive KYC program, including information about their financial situation, investment experience and investment objectives relevant to the services to be provided.
	• Safeguarding of confidential information.
	• Advice to clients regarding their behaviour.
	• Make appropriate disclosures.
	• Ensure that client assets are promptly and properly accounted for and adequately safeguarded.

3. Communication with Regulators
	• Openly cooperate with regulators and consult them early when an issue or potential issue is identified.
	• Dealing with the regulators should be done by the appropriate staff.

4. Personal account dealings
	• Policy and procedures to guide, prohibit and monitor personal account dealings.

5. Compliance
	• Comply with all regulatory requirements applicable to the conduct of its business activities.
---- 
> ### Key Learning Point
> 
> Professional accountants in Hong Kong are subject to multiple codes of conduct and regulation. It is important to be familiar with the scope, structure, requirements and purpose of each code.
> The various codes offer detailed requirements, general or conceptual fundamentals, as well as guidance on applying the codes in practice.
> Professionals should complete relevant training and utilise other resources to gain the knowledge and skills to comply with the requirements.

### Knowledge Check Questions

Question 6 Identify which one of the following sets out the requirements and guidance to ensure sound and effective internal controls are in place to protect the stakeholder’s interests. 
A SFO Code 
B CFA Code 
C Takeover Code 
D Corporate Governance Code

# SUMMARY
• The major regulatory authorities relevant to a professional accountant in Hong Kong are the: 
- HKICPA: regulates the accounting profession. 
- SFC: regulates the securities and futures market in Hong Kong. 
- FRC: investigates and enquires into potential auditing and financial reporting irregularities. 
- HKMA: is the central bank and is responsible for stability of the financial system. 
- IA: regulates the insurance industry. 
- HKEx: operates exchanges and clearing activities and is regulated by the SFC.
• It is important for professional accountants in Hong Kong to be familiar with the scope, structure, requirements and purpose of the multiple codes of conduct and regulation.
• Professionals should complete relevant training and utilise other resources to gain the knowledge and skills to comply with the requirements.

## Past paper

### Answer 1(a)

#### Advantages for companies to list their shares:

##### Access to capital 
It is easier for companies to obtain capital for growth both at the time of listing (immediately) and at later stages.

##### Increase corporate transparency 
Since companies must comply with the listing rules and regulations, as well as the disclosure requirements, it is easier for companies to obtain credit lines or obtain loans on more competitive terms from bankers.

##### Improve the image and reputation of companies 
The operations and governances of the companies become more transparent, which can improve the image and reputation of the companies.

#### Disadvantages for companies to list their shares:

##### Less flexible in doing business 
Companies are less flexible in doing business as they must comply with rigorous listing rules and requirements.

##### Threat of being taken over 
Investors can acquire substantial shares to take over the companies or control the companies.

##### Information disclosed to competitors 
With important or sensitive information being disclosed, such as plans for selling certain businesses, plans for expansion, and plans for acquiring other companies etc., competitors may utilise such information to put the companies at a disadvantaged position.

### Answer 1(b)(i)

The listing criteria of the Group as a whole to be listed on the Main Board of the HKEx: 

#### Trading record 
The Group was established in 2010, and therefore satisfies the requirement of not less than three financial years.

#### Minimum continuity of ownership and management 
Mr Woo and key owners have owned and controlled the Group since the establishment, satisfying the continuity of ownership and control requirement for at least the most recent audited financial year AND the continuity of management requirement for at least three preceding financial years.

The management should also consider the following matters in order to satisfy the listing requirements:

Management presence in Hong Kong Mr Woo and key owners stay in Hong Kong for most of the time to oversee the daily operation of the business; however, the management should consider whether there are at least two executive directors who are resident in Hong Kong.

#### Financial year 
The management of the Group should ensure that the period of its financial year should be the same as HKEx will not normally consider a new applicant suitable if the Group has changed the period of its financial year recently.

#### Financial requirement 
The management of the Group should review the financial requirement and must pass one of the three tests (profit test or market capitalisation/ revenue/ cash flow test or market capitalisation/ revenue test).

#### Underwriting requirement 
The management of the Group should ensure the issue should be fully underwritten in order to satisfy the listing criteria.

The listing criteria of Ultimate Biotech HK Limited to be listed as a Biotech Company under Chapter 18A on the Main Board of the HKEx:

#### Minimum continuity of management
Ultimate Biotech HK Limited has been in operation in its current line of business since 2016, satisfying the minimum continuity of management for at least two financial years prior to listing under substantially the same management.

Eligibility Ultimate Biotech HK Limited must demonstrate to the HKEx that it is both eligible and suitable for listing as a Biotech Company.

The management should also consider the following matters in order to satisfy the listing requirements:

Initial market capitalisation The management should ensure that Ultimate Biotech HK Limited must have an initial market capitalisation of at least HK$1,500 million at the time of listing.

Financial requirement The management should ensure that Ultimate Biotech HK Limited has sufficient working capital to cover at least 125% of the Group's costs for at least 12 months from the date of publication of its listing document (after taking into account the proceeds of the new applicant's initial listing). These costs must substantially consist of the general, administrative and operating costs as well as the research and development costs.

Other requirements Besides satisfying the above conditions, Ultimate Biotech HK Limited must also satisfy the requirements of Chapter 8 (except the rules 8.05, 8.05A, 8.05B, and 8.05C).
