### 8.3.1 Balanced Scorecard (BSC)

The most well-known and widely used multidimensional performance measurement system is the balanced scorecard (BSC).

The rationale for the development of the BSC was a growing dissatisfaction with traditional, purely financial measures of performance.

By incorporating both financial and non-financial measures, the BSC endeavours to provide management with a multi-dimensional set of information covering all relevant areas of performance in an objective and unbiased fashion.

The original Kaplan and Norton model contains four groupings of performance measures. These groupings, collectively referred to as ‘perspectives’ were considered by Kaplan and Norton to be sufficient to track the key drivers of both current and future financial performance of the firm.

The perspectives focus on the achievements of the organisation in four areas:

1. Financial: Similar to traditional performance measures, this focuses on how the organisation performs in terms of return to shareholders. Typical metrics used to assess whether the organisation’s financial objectives are being achieved and include profitability, return on investment, return on assets, liquidity and cash flow, residual income and economic value added.

2. Customer: The metrics selected for this perspective are designed to determine what the organisation must do to satisfy its customers so as to achieve its financial objectives. Metrics typically include measures of customer satisfaction, such as, market share, customer retention and customer profitability.

	These can be sub-divided into measures that drive customer satisfaction, including lead times, on-time delivery, product quality and product cost.

3. Internal processes: This perspective focuses on identifying and measuring what the organisation must excel at in order to successfully support its product/marketing strategies and thereby achieve its financial objectives.

	Typical outcome measures include those relating to innovation (product and process) and operations (cycle times, defect rates).

4. Learning and growth: These measures are designed to ascertain what infrastructure and knowledge base the firm must establish, build and maintain to create long-term growth and organisational improvement.

	In other words, what capabilities must be improved or acquired to achieve the longterm targets for the organisation and internal business process perspectives. Outcome measures typically include metrics on employee satisfaction, training and staff retention.

In constructing a balanced scorecard, careful consideration needs to be given to the selection of meaningful metrics for each of the perspectives (traditionally four, but see Section 8.3.1.1 for emerging trends toward the addition of a fifth perspective).

In selecting metrics, it is important to include an appropriate mix of ‘input’ and ‘output’ measures. Input measures (usually referred to as ‘leading’ indicators) are typically quite hard to measure but relatively easy to influence. Conversely, output measures (usually referred to as ‘lagging’ indicators’) are usually easy to measure but hard to improve.

Put another way, leading indicators usually cause some change or effect in one or more lagging indicators.

Whilst the BSC can be visually represented in many ways, a typical schematic is shown in Exhibit 8.8.
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-34.png)
Objective, KPI/ measures, Targets, Initiatives

#### Apply and Analyse 2

Daphne Zhou, the CFO of EasyTrip, a medium size, family owned, Hong Kong-based travel agency with outlets in Hong Kong, Macau, Beijing, Shanghai and Chongqing, is considering introducing the balanced scorecard in her organisation. Currently, performance measurement at EasyTrip is based almost entirely on summary financial measures such as gross margin per trip, earnings before interest and tax (EBIT) and return on investment (ROI). Daphne, who is based at the head office in Hong Kong, has recently been worried about her organisation’s deteriorating financial performance and has also become aware that a number of customer complaints have been made about the quality of service at some of EasyTrips outlets. Daphne is also aware that EasyTrip’s market share appears to be deteriorating in what is a very competitive market.

Required:

(a) Identify and explain three problems associated with the use of non-financial performance measures.

(b) Distinguish between lead and lag performance measures. To illustrate, identify one lead measure and one lag measure that would be suitable for Daphne’s organisation for the customer perspective of the balanced scorecard. Justify your choices.

(c) Daphne attended a business conference last week and a CEO from another organisation was talking about cause and effect linkages within the balanced scorecard. Daphne had no idea what he was talking about. Explain to Daphne how cause and effect linkages exist between the four (traditional) perspectives of the balanced scorecard, using examples to illustrate.

Analysis

#### (a) Some problems associated with the use of non-financial performance measures are:
• **Wide choice** of non-financial measures available – selecting too many could be problematic. BSC should be kept as simple as possible to avoid it becoming unwieldy and difficult to use effectively.
• Their development can be **ad-hoc and undirected** – this frequently happens when new staff are appointed.
• Managers must necessarily make **trade-offs** between achieving some measures and not others – this can cause tensions between different parts of the organisation.
• Some measures **lack integrity/accuracy** of the data, creating an opportunity for manipulation.
• Some measures are **not easily translated** into financial outcomes.

#### (b) Characteristics and examples of lead and lag indicators:
##### (i) Lead indicators 
They are measures that drive the outcomes and provide information that is actionable and manageable.

They relate to the processes and activities of the business. Improvements in these measures should, over time, flow through to improvements in lag indicators.

Examples: Number of customer complaints, number of repeat customers, number of refund demands.

##### (ii) Lag indicators
They monitor progress towards the organisation’s objectives.
Examples: customer satisfaction (survey), industry market share data.

#### (c) Causal linkages connect objectives and measures in the balanced scorecard. 
In effect, they identify an interconnected pathway between non-financial measures of performance, and the ultimate and collective impact of all of those on the overall financial performance of the organisation.

In the case of EasyTrip, good performance on measures in the learning and growth perspective (i.e. improved tourism planning and vacation-sales skills) should then drive good performance in the internal business process perspectives (for example, increased margins on trips, more and longer vacations booked, more billable tourism experiences, more lucrative partnerships with hotels and tour operators). In turn, these improved service attributes should lead to good performance in relation to the customer perspective (for example by making the travel experiences more reliable, more customised, more attractive to customers etc.). Finally, this should lead to improved financial performance through increased sales, lower cancelation rates and increased repeat business and referral business that should then reflect in better margins and overall financial performance.

#### 8.3.1.2 Advantages
Correctly designed, implemented, monitored and actioned, the BSC offers the following advantages:
1. It measures performance across multiple dimensions, rather than relying solely on a limited range of financial indicators.
2. Managers will find it difficult to distort the performance measure – poor performance is hard to disguise or hide when multiple measures are used.
3. It takes a long-term perspective in assessing organisational performance.
4. Success in each of the four perspectives should result in delivering long-term accomplishment of organisational objectives.
5. It is flexible – what is measured can be changed over time to reflect changing competitive, market and environmental conditions and organisational priorities.
6. ’What gets measured gets done’ – if managers know they are being appraised on various aspects of performance they are more likely to properly focus their energies on these and not become distracted by other, less important matters.

#### 8.3.1.3 Disadvantages
Notwithstanding its potential value as a way of expressing organisation vision and strategy into specific objectives, and quantified performance measures (which have doubtlessly contributed to its widespread acceptance and adoption in many businesses), the BSC certainly has its critics, with research suggesting that some BSC implementations have not been successful. Many of the reasons for this suggested outcome relate to problems with implementation rather than conceptual validity. These problems are discussed further in Section 8.4.

From a practical perspective, the main difficulty with the BSC approach is setting standards for each of the key performance indicators (KPIs). This can prove difficult where the organisation has no previous experience of performance measurement. Benchmarking with other organisations is a possible solution to this problem, although it can be difficult to obtain the necessary information.

# 8.4 Implementation
Section 8.3 discussed the changes that have occurred over recent decades in PMS and introduced three of the better known multidimensional frameworks that have evolved during this period. Against this background, this section first addresses some of the practical issues associated with selecting and implementing these kinds of systems. This is followed by a discussion of the processes and systems for employee performance evaluation which frequently form an important element of any PMS. Next, a brief review of the roles of an organisation’s board of directors’ governance and risk management committees is presented to demonstrate how the oversight provided by these bodies can contribute to the overall effectiveness of an organisation’s PMS.

Broadly stated, PMSs serve two main functions: to ensure that organisations are held accountable for their performance and actions and to facilitate improved performance through organisational development and learning. These two functions can sometimes be at odds with each other, making designing a PMS in a way that facilitates achievement of these two sometimes conflicting objectives quite difficult. Selection of an appropriate range of individual performance measures can be challenging given the need to satisfy both objectives and to then integrate the measures selected into a cohesive multi-dimensional PMS that supports overall organisational strategy.

These design challenges are compounded because the PMS operates within and across both the internal and external environment within which an organisation operates and should ideally be designed in a way that ensures that it remains relevant and useful as the environment changes and the organisation evolves.

Internally, the PMS forms part of the strategy development, goal setting and reward processes of the company and should ideally reflect the prevailing organisational culture.

The external environment typically comprises customers, suppliers, competitors and regulators.

## 8.4.1 Benefits and Problems

Otley, Berry and Broadbent (1995) identified the following potential benefits from implementing and making effective use of performance measures:

• Clarifies organisational objectives.
• Facilitates development of relevant activity measures.
• Helps establish and disseminate understanding of organisational processes.
• Provides a basis for intra (and possibly inter) organisational performance measures.
• Establishes a framework for the setting of relevant targets – both for the organisation and for individual departments and managers.
• Can foster a culture of accountability within the organisation.

However, the authors also drew attention to the following potential problems:
• Tunnel vision – Focusing too much on some performance measures to the detriment of others.
• Sub-optimisation – Concentrating too much on one or more measures may result in others not being achieved.
• Myopia – A shortsighted approach that can lead to longer term objectives being ignored.
• Misrepresentation – There can be a tendency to construct performance reports in a way that suggests a particular performance measure outcome is satisfactory when in fact it may not be.
• Misinterpretation – Sometimes staff will not properly understand the complexities present in the organisational environment.
• Gaming – Describes situations where one or more staff may deliberately distort the reporting and/or interpretation of a performance measure to obtain a strategic or operational advantage over another area.

It is important that management carefully consider whether there are likely to be problems in using performance measures as targets for the organisation and its managers. Poorly conceived and implemented targets can adversely affect morale and thus potentially negatively impact performance. This raises the question of the compatibility (congruence) of individual and organisational goals which in practice can be difficult to reconcile.

Careful assessment of whether the use of targets will help to provide accountability of the organisation and its employees to all stakeholder groups should therefore be of paramount concern when designing the PMS. Individual goals may focus on financial and non-financial areas such as remuneration, promotion prospects, job security, job satisfaction and self-esteem. Whilst these seem intuitively reasonable, they may conflict with actions that may be required to achieve broader organisational goals.

## 8.4.2 Design of the Performance Measurement System

In designing the PMS, management needs to consider the following key design parameters:
• Ensuring that performance measures link to organisational strategy.
• Identifying appropriate performance standards and targets.
• Being aware of the potential benefits and drawbacks of the measures selected.
• Understanding how to link reward systems to individual and organisational performance.

To establish a clear link between performance and strategy, it is necessary to ensure that the performance measures chosen **target areas within the business where success is a critical factor.** The performance measures chosen should therefore be able to:
• Comprise an appropriate mix of both quantitative and qualitative methods.
• Comprise an appropriate focus on both the long-term and short-term.
• Measure efficiency in terms of resource utilisation within the organisation.
• Measure the effectiveness of all processes (including products and/or services) that reach the customer.
• Be flexible and adaptable to an ever-changing business environment.

The last point stresses the importance of ensuring that the PMS is dynamic so that it will be able to remain relevant and continue to reflect the issues important to the organisation. The frameworks discussed in Section 8.3 (balanced scorecard, performance pyramid, building block model) have all been designed to provide this flexibility.

In addition to the overarching parameters set out at the beginning of this section, the PMS should:
• Include a system of regular feedback and review of actual results against the original plan and the performance measures themselves.
• Be comprehensive and include financial and non-financial factors that contribute to the organisation’s success. These could include competitive performance, quality of service and innovation.
• Be ‘owned’ and supported throughout the entire organisation. Ideally, the design and implementation phases should be ‘top-down’ so that the senior managers responsible for setting strategy can determine the correct objectives and develop appropriate top-level metrics. These should be communicated to the lower levels of the organisation in a transparent and collaborative way. Once this has been accomplished, lower levels throughout the organisation should then be actively involved in setting their own measures. To ensure that these are consistent with the top-level objectives, this process should be carried out in consultation with the relevant higher-level managers.
• Be fair and achievable. Where performance measures are used to reward managers’ performance, the applicable part of the PMS evaluation should include only elements over which the staff have direct control.
• Be simple, clear and understandable, particularly to non-finance staff.

## 8.4.3 Problems in Performance Measurement

Organisations which fail to utilise performance measurement effectively tend to fall into two categories:
• Those who continue to employ the traditional, financial, accounting-based performance measures.
• Those who have failed to implement properly or revise appropriately their performance measurement system that includes multidimensional and non-financial factors such as those discussed in Section 8.3.

The first category should widen their approach by including non-financial and multidimensional performance measures.

Both categories should be aware of the following issues when trying to use or implement a multidimensional performance measurement system:
• Difficulty in measuring key success drivers, such as innovation or flexibility, leading to the temptation to avoid measuring them.
• Employees’ behaviour not being aligned to strategic objectives. This can occur when the strategy, objectives and target setting processes are not developed in an open and collaborative way.
• The possibility of, and, if present, the nature of any PMS conflicting with the culture of the organisation.

• Not allowing sufficient time and resources for the project. This can result in short cuts being taken because the process is too time-consuming or difficult. A systematic and logical approach is required, together with a recognition that achieving an equitable and useful PMS will usually take quite some time.

## 8.4.4 Performance Evaluation Process

The performance evaluation process (also called a performance appraisal, or a performance review) can be defined as the various techniques used to judge an employee’s work performance to objectively assess the effectiveness by which the employee carries out the assigned tasks. It is a process for establishing a shared understanding of desired organisational achievements and involves the alignment of organisational objectives with the individual’s agreed performance measures, skills, competencies, development plans and key performance indicators. The emphasis is on performance improvement through learning and development to accomplish the overall strategy of the organisation.

Performance evaluation integrates a multitude of elements that contribute to effective management of an organisation’s human resources. To be effective, performance evaluation needs to be a systematic process which a manager can use to encourage team members to achieve the team’s objectives and targets, improve overall team effectiveness, develop performance capabilities, review and assess team and individual performance and reward and motivate.

Effective performance evaluation requires:
• Identifying tasks, responsibilities and accountabilities for each employee (or category of employee).
• Defining competencies necessary to be successful in a position.
• Ensuring that employees have the competencies required to effectively perform their duties.
• Ensuring systems are in place to develop required competencies.
• Providing timely feedback on how effectively employees are applying their respective competencies to accomplish their tasks and achieve specified goals.
• Rewarding and motivating effective performance.
• An understanding of the corrective processes and methods that can help improve employee performance for application in circumstances where an employee’s performance does not meet established requirements.

The overarching goal of these processes is to provide an equitable measurement of an employee’s contribution to the workforce, utilising accurate appraisal documentation to protect both the employee and employer, to maximise the likelihood of obtaining a high level of quality and quantity in the work produced.

A performance evaluation typically comprises oral and written components, through which management evaluates and provides feedback on employee job performance and, if necessary, suggests ways in which the employee might be able to improve, refocus or redirect his or her activities to the benefit of the individual, the department and the whole organisation. Careful and objective discussion and detailed documentation are used to provide a basis for remuneration increases, increased responsibility, promotions and (possibly) other benefits. Evaluations also assist employees to improve their understanding of the ‘big picture’ and the way in which their role forms part of the broader organisational jigsaw. They can also serve a range of other functions and provide a starting point for companies to explain, and if necessary, clarify and shape employee responsibilities in accordance with emerging business trends and improve communications between employees and management.

While the term ‘performance evaluation’ may mean slightly different things for individual organisations, common goals are likely to include:
• To improve the company’s productivity.
• To make informed personnel decisions regarding promotion, job changes and termination.
• To identify what is required to perform the goals and responsibilities of the position.
• To equitably and objectively assess an employee’s performance against these goals.
• To work collaboratively to improve the employee’s performance by identifying specific areas for improvement, developing a plan aimed at improving these areas, supporting the employee’s efforts at improvement by giving timely and objective feedback and assistance, and ensuring the employee’s involvement and commitment to improving his or her performance.

These goals are more likely to be accomplished if the employer’s endeavours to establish the performance appraisal process as a collaborative dialogue in which the ultimate purpose is the betterment of all parties.

To create and maintain this kind of framework, it is important that employers inform workers of their value, praise them for their accomplishments, establish a track record of providing fair and honest feedback, be consistent in their treatment of all employees and seek workers’ own insights into the company’s processes and operations.

The size and structure of the organisation is likely to influence the type of system introduced. A small business with relatively few employees or one that is only just introducing a formal evaluation system may choose to use a ‘prepackaged’ evaluation system, perhaps consisting of pre-printed forms or ‘off the shelf’ software. Most software packages allow at least some level of customisation either by using the organisation’s existing appraisal parameters or by selecting elements from a list of attributes that describe a successful employee’s work habits such as timeliness, ability to perform the designated work and ability to communicate effectively. However, many companies eventually choose to reflect their own goals and culture when developing their own appraisal system. In developing a performance evaluation system, management would typically need to consider the following:
• Number of staff.
• Employees on ‘non-standard’ working arrangements.
• Aligning organisational goals with employee behaviour.
• Measuring employee performance/productivity.
• Provide a basis for reviewing remuneration, promotional opportunities and other benefits.
• Effective and transparent communication of the process to employees.

### 8.4.4.1 Number of Staff

A small business with relatively few employees may choose to use an informal approach when assessing employee performance. As an example, this could involve meeting with each employee once or twice per year to discuss the individual’s work performance and progress since the previous meeting. Whilst feedback can be verbal, as a minimum, it is usually beneficial to maintain notes of the discussion to avoid any future misunderstanding about what was said and agreed.

As a company grows and employee numbers increase, a more formal system using a written appraisal form should preferably be used. The documentation could have been designed internally or externally, depending on the specific requirements and expertise available. The linkage between performance and remuneration should be clearly explained in the documentation.

### 8.4.4.2 Non-standard Working Arrangements

Employees working on some form of ‘non-standard’ basis, for example working at home, working part time, job sharing, etc., may need to have their performance evaluated differently from the regular full-time staff. Non-standard working arrangements may have different requirements regarding aspects of performance which should be incorporated into the evaluation process.

### 8.4.4.3 Aligning Organisational Goals with Employee Behaviour

The performance of employees is an essential determinant of the ability of the organisation to achieve its objectives. In smaller organisations, goal setting and accomplishment usually consists of making sure that words are transformed into actions, but in larger organisations the process is more complex, requiring clear delineation of each employees’ responsibilities and the role that each employee plays in the broader organisational setting. Importantly, the evaluation system should incorporate factors such as collaborative ability and sense of teamwork, not only individual performance.

### 8.4.4.4 Measuring Employee Performance/Productivity 
Once a comprehensive list of duties and responsibilities has been developed, management needs to determine how to actually measure an employee’s performance of those duties. Measurement is required to provide objectivity in the evaluation process. Ideally, measurement would be made against previous performance, whether of the individual employee, the department or the entire organisation. Whilst difficult and potentially controversial, every effort needs to be made to ensure that performance metrics are as realistic as possible.

### 8.4.4.5 Reviewing Remuneration, Promotion Opportunities and Other Benefits

Because of its importance to employees, this is arguably one of the most important aspects of the entire performance evaluation system. It is essential that management carefully consider the relationship between the evaluation itself and any increases in remuneration, promotion opportunities and other benefits.

While performance feedback for development/improvement purposes may be given verbally, it is essential that a written statement detailing the individual’s work performance should accompany the granting (or deferral) of any change to remuneration. Unfortunately, it is sometimes necessary for employees to be cautioned, or even have their jobs terminated for performance-related shortcomings. In these situations, a written history is essential to minimise the likelihood of any dispute arising.

8.4.4.6 Effective and Transparent Communication of the Process to Employees

A performance evaluation system can only be effective if it is properly communicated and fully understood by all employees. When developing an evaluation system management may wish to seek input from staff – or at least some of them. Inviting participation in the process in this way is likely to improve employee understanding and acceptance of the process. While this assessment may be written, it should, if possible, also be explained verbally, preferably face to face. This provides management with the opportunity to answer any questions the employee may have, and to also provide more information and clarification should this be necessary.

### 8.4.4.7 Types of Performance Evaluation Systems

Several types of performance evaluation systems exist. These include traditional evaluations, self-appraisals, employee-initiated reviews and 360-degree feedback. The following sections explore each of these.

#### Traditional

In a traditional performance evaluation, the employee’s line manager will meet face to face with the employee and discuss performance for the preceding period. The frequency of these meetings can vary but would ordinarily take place at least once per year. The discussion is based on the manager’s observations of the employee’s abilities and performance of tasks as noted in the employees’ statement of duties, or job description as it is frequently called. The performance is rated, with the ratings tied to salary percentage increases. However, the traditional merit raise process usually grants even poor performers an automatic cost of living increase, thereby creating perceived inequity. Furthermore, most traditional performance appraisals form trends that use too many rating categories and distribute ratings using a forced-distribution format. Current trends suggest that some simplification, perhaps based on as few as three rating categories – outstanding, fully competent and unsatisfactory – may be a better approach as it would enable managers to assess their best and worst employees, with the rest falling somewhere in between.

#### Self-Appraisal

As the name indicates, the self-appraisal approach is used in the performance evaluation to encourage staff members to take responsibility for their own performance by assessing their own achievements or failures and promoting self-management of individual development goals. It also imposes a discipline on the employee to ensure that they are as well prepared as possible to objectively discuss performance with their manager. It may be used in conjunction with or as a part of a wider evaluation process but would not ordinarily substitute for an evaluation of the employee’s performance by their manager.

#### Employee-Initiated Reviews

An employee-initiated evaluation review system provides employees with the opportunity to request a meeting with their manager to discuss their performance. This form of evaluation is additional to the normal process and should not be conducted to replace whatever routine processes are in place. Supporters of this form of evaluation believe that it encourages regular communication between employees and their managers. On the other hand, those who are not in favour of this type of process argue that whilst it may be suitable for more confident, outgoing employees, it is likely to be less suitable for employees with less confidence, thereby creating some potential inequities in the overall way the employee is assessed.

#### 360-Degree Feedback

360-degree feedback is a popular feedback tool that provides employees with the opportunity to not only receive feedback from their manager, but also from several of their peers. This could typically involve up to eight other people, including direct reports, other co-workers and even other stakeholders such as customers and/or suppliers. Collectively, these are frequently called ‘raters’. Most 360-degree feedback tools also provide the opportunity for the individual being assessed to respond in self-assessment.

360-degree feedback allows each employee to gain an understanding of how his or her effectiveness as an employee, co-worker or staff member is viewed by others. The most effective 360-degree feedback processes provide feedback that is based on behaviours that other employees can observe.

Importantly, carefully constructed and implemented, the 360-degree process provides insight into the skills and behaviours desired to accomplish the mission and goals of the organisation and to provide a platform for employees to adhere to the ‘values’ of the organisation.

To encourage objectivity and transparency, those selected as raters are often chosen in a shared process by both the organisation and the employee. The purpose of the 360-degree feedback is to assist everyone in understanding their strengths and weaknesses and to contribute insights into aspects of their work needing professional development. To ensure that staff feel able to speak openly and truthfully, maintaining confidentiality through the entire process is extremely important. Whilst increasingly widespread in practice, in addition to confidentially, great care needs to be taken when considering how to:

• Select the feedback tool and process.
• Choose the raters.
• Use the feedback.
• Review the feedback.
• Manage and integrate the process into a larger performance management system.

## 8.4.5 Approval Process

The immediately preceding sections have provided a ‘how to’ overview of performance measurement and evaluation, addressing the implementation process from the perspectives of selection (first) and evaluation (second). Given their importance, these interrelated stages need to be undertaken in a systematic and transparent way and to also be subjected to appropriate checks and balances prior to being formally approved.

As these systems usually form an overarching ‘umbrella’ impacting all aspects of the organisation, final approval of these systems will ordinarily rest with the board of directors.

To ensure that the fiduciary and other responsibilities of the board of directors are performed in the most effective manner, in most organisations the board will establish a series of committees, each charged with responsibilities for oversight of various aspects of the organisation’s activities. Whilst the number of committees will vary to reflect organisational size and complexity, the most common of these include the governance committee, the risk management committee, the audit committee and the remuneration committee.

Whilst there is no regulatory requirement dictating that this should be the case, responsibility for the approval and oversight of performance measurement systems would most probably vest with the governance committee.

### 8.4.5.1 Governance Committee 
Managerial attitudes, behaviour and actions collectively influence organisational performance in both positive and negative ways. Ideally managers should carry out their duties in the best interests of shareholders, but sometimes, for a variety of reasons, there may be reasons (and possibly incentives) that result in this not being the case. Because of this, it is important that appropriate processes and controls are in place to prudently monitor management activities, including the design, implementation and use of PMSs.

Collectively, these processes and controls constitute corporate governance. As an overarching concept, corporate governance provides a mechanism to separate ownership and control. Ideally, this should include the separation of roles and responsibilities, communication channels and behaviour between shareholders, board(s) of directors (both executives and non-executives) and the CEO.

#### Approaches to Governance 
The interrelationships between these different stakeholder groups is often examined through an agency theory perspective, but stewardship theory and stakeholder theory are sometimes used. These theories are neither mutually exclusive nor collectively exhaustive, but they can help explain the linkage between proper governance and organisational performance. To provide context, each is now briefly discussed.

Agency theory is predicated on the assumption that there can frequently be a conflict of interest between the various parties ‘connected’ to the organisation. This can lead to the emergence of an agency problem if a principal (for example a shareholder) employs an agent (such as the CEO) to manage the organisation on behalf of the principal.

The agency theory approach to corporate governance proffers that ‘rule setting’, monitoring and incentive and sanctioning mechanisms are necessary to ensure alignment between the interests of both principal and agent.

Stewardship theory is based on the premise that management should always put the long-term best interests of a ‘group’ ahead of any individual self-interest. Stewards, unlike agents, consider their interests to be aligned with the interests of the organisation and its owners. As managers, stewards are well-positioned to maximise the interests of all stakeholders, including shareholders, since they are most familiar with the strengths, weaknesses, opportunities and threats facing the organisation.

From a governance perspective, stewardship theory stresses the importance of selecting and training competent, reliable and honest managers so that all parties work towards a common goal without taking advantage of their access to confidential privileged information.

Stakeholder theory assumes that optimal organisational performance depends on the collective and synergistic contributions of many different parties. These ‘stakeholders’ (shareholders as well as other parties such as employees, customers, suppliers and regulators) not only have a stake in the organisation but can also decide how to engage based on the information available to them. Against this potentially diverse range of interested parties, management has the very challenging responsibility to balance sometimes-conflicting objectives. This is made even more difficult because it is likely that at the same time, stakeholders will be trying to persuade management that their set of objectives, goals etc., should take priority over others.

Stakeholder theory thus argues that effective corporate governance is required to ensure that all perspectives are considered and communicated equitably to all stakeholders.

#### Developing a Governance Model

Whilst there is no ‘right’ or ‘best’ way to develop a governance model, in practice common themes regarding how to approach this task have emerged along the following lines:
• Define business goals and objectives.
• Evaluate organisational resources.
• Plan the necessary processes.
• Decide who should lead the governance process.
• Create appropriate and scalable documentation and processes.
• Plan and initiate appropriate training for staff.

### 8.4.5.2 Risk Management Committee

In today’s increasingly competitive and litigious business environment, organisations are facing increasing pressure from stakeholders to ensure that risks are managed effectively. As a consequence, corporate governance practices are developing with risk management as a priority. Whilst risk management has traditionally typically vested with the audit committee, an increasing number of companies are now establishing dedicated risk management committees tasked with ensuring that risk management processes are clearly documented and transparently communicated to various stakeholders.

Whilst there is no specific or universal model detailing the responsibilities of the risk management committee, the following model would be typical:

The risk management committee will provide advice and assistance to the board in its governance responsibilities by monitoring and advising on the management of:
• Business risks, including financial and strategic risks;
• Operational risks, including business continuity and practice management risks;
• Insurable risks including legal liability claims and property losses;
• Environmental, social and governance risks; and
• Internal controls and treatments for identified risks including the company’s insurance requirements

The risk management committee should also provide a forum for communication between the board, senior management and external risk management advisors and provide a clear and readily accessible conduit to the board for external advice on risk management.

To accomplish this, risk management and performance measurement should be linked together to define and guide the organisations’ overall risk profile, as well as contribute to framing the organisation’s overall strategic direction.

Effectively linked, performance management and risk management can help management and the board understand how well their risk management framework and associated processes are working. Properly designed PMSs should be able to ensure that risk management can be continually monitored and improved, thereby providing senior management with the means to focus attention on areas of current and potential risk exposure and areas for improvement.

Measuring and monitoring risk management effectiveness is no different from measuring other categories of performance; measures are identified and expected targets or benchmarks are developed. As with other performance measures, risk management indicators can take qualitative and/or quantitative forms. Qualitative measures are based on subjective characteristics or qualities rather than on a quantity or measured value. Conversely, quantitative measures are based on objective, quantifiable data, like percentages, counts and ratios. The difference between qualitative and quantitative measures can sometimes be subtle, which can lead to mixed opinions as to which approach is more useful. In practice, a combination of both is frequently adopted.

Senior management and boards are aware that all PMSs will influence organisational behaviour. Ideally, PMSs should be designed to be predictive as well as prescriptive to capture and collate information that will be of value in managing performance. Developing a risk management plan is complex and challenging due to its predictive and inexact nature.

A business risk management plan involves identifying, assessing and developing strategies to manage risks and will be an important component of any set of PMS. An essential pre-requisite to developing a risk management plan is for the organisation to have a clear understanding of its risk appetite. Broadly stated, risk appetite reflects the level of risk that the organisation is prepared to tolerate in pursuing its goals before intervention is deemed necessary to minimse the risk. A risk management plan will typically include:

• Details of the risks identified;
• Some measure regarding level of each class of risk;
• Planned strategies to manage those risks;
• The time frame for implementing each strategy;
• The resources required; and
• The staff responsible for ensuring the strategies are implemented.

## 8.4.6 Periodic Review and Recommendations

Given the dynamic nature of today’s business environment, most organisations will need to continually respond to the changes occurring in their sphere of operations. Implicitly, this suggests that changes will also be necessary in the structure and composition of the PMSs in place. For this reason, some form of periodic review of the current PMS becomes a necessity.

For a review of the PMS to be effective, it must be able to identify any flaws and weaknesses in the current PMS and recommend plausible corrections, amendments and necessary changes.

Some typical weaknesses that can hinder the effectiveness of review processes include:
• Unproductive and long management meetings, which result in little or no effective action.
• Strategic measures that are not connected to operational issues and are not relevant to the daily pressures to ‘deliver the budget’.
• There is little insight derived from the measures, even when there seems to be more than enough data.
• Targets that are arbitrary affect the behaviour of staff negatively and damage the way staff and managers view their work and the organisation.
• Overall organisational performance suffers from sub-optimal performance.
• Individual fears of negative exposure result in facts being concealed and prevent the creation of an open-learning culture.

These weaknesses could be mitigated by adopting the following actions and recommendations.

1. Optimise the value of measurement by including measures that matter to all stakeholders.
2. Present data visually to aid interpretation and thereby derive maximum value from the data, for example, present large volumes of data in a ‘performance dashboard’ format to highlight the few insights likely to be economically worthy of attention.
3. Structure the review process properly, ensuring that there is a clear definition of who needs to come together to review ‘what, where, when, why and how’, and be mindful that the frequency of review is critical. Typically, the faster the cycle of review, the faster the rate of progress.
4. Ensure that review meetings deliver value. The person accountable must make the review process matter and ensure that no arbitrary numerical targets are set (goals are fine, if the culture promotes root-cause analysis, problem-solving and innovation, whereas arbitrary numerical targets are usually not).
5. Connect the review process between levels to promote problem-solving at the local level.
6. Tackle implementation progressively. Implementation will succeed and take root if the approach adopted is more like doing a jigsaw (i.e. tackling the easiest pieces first) than moving forward on a broad front.

These points may appear to be obvious initially. However, optimising the review process frequently occurs last in most organisations. Usually, the process of defining and implementing their new measures tire most senior managers, or any offer of help with their review process is regarded as critical of their managerial skills.

### Apply and Analyse 4

Creative Furnishing Imports (CFI) is a 100% family owned business based in Hong Kong that specialises in importing high-end furniture and objects d’art from Europe for high net worth expatriates living in several major cities across the Asian region. The chief executive and chairman is H.K. (Howard) Lim, a charismatic and energetic businessman with an uncanny ability to identify market trends in the very competitive interior design business. Established 10 years ago, the company has been profitable almost since day one. Howard’s wife and two sons are also directors of the company, but none of them plays an active role in the company’s day to day management. Consequently, Howard makes virtually all major decisions. Now in his late fifties, Howard is starting to think about the future and is starting to contemplate various exit strategies. The two most likely options are a management buyout by some of his senior employees, or perhaps a sale to an outside company. A less likely scenario would be an IPO.

Reflecting on each of these options Howard is aware that it may be prudent to review, and possibly strengthen, the company’s corporate governance practices. However, Howard’s wife and their two sons did not think this was necessary and that it would just create more paperwork and red tape, and possibly even reduce profitability. The different views amongst the family members were made worse when Howard suggested that he was also considering a new incentive scheme, in which he proposed issuing shares to 5 of the senior staff as he was convinced that this would motivate those staff members and help make sure that their goals were aligned with his own – namely exiting the business in the next three years or so. When challenged by his family about why he would do this, Howard explained that the shares would be issued subject to achieving some challenging financial performance targets.

For each of the following three questions, assume that Howard has sought your advice to help him explain to his wife and two sons his proposed changes to governance and incentives:

(a) Identify and describe the main differences between governance and management of a company.

(b) Explain, with reasons, whether governance imposes bureaucracy on a company and stifles innovation.

(c) Discuss the possible implications of the proposed policy of rewarding senior executives with grants of shares in the company.

Analysis

(a) The role of corporate governance is to establish and monitor the company’s overall values and structure and to provide oversight and decision-making related to strategic direction. The governance body (typically the governance committee) has no role in day to day management. Its purpose is to oversee the functioning of management, who have the responsibility to conduct the routine day to day operations of the company. To accomplish this, the governance process itself comprises various systems and controls by which company executives and managers are expected to manage the company.

More simply put, management is about running the company, whereas governance is about ensuring that it is run properly, in the interest of shareholders and other stakeholders.

Effective governance seeks to satisfy the (sometimes conflicting) objectives of shareholders and other stakeholders, and to provide regular information that comprehensively and transparently shows how this is being achieved.

(b) Developing, implementing and ensuring ongoing compliance with corporate governance principles and provisions is sometimes subjected to the criticism that it is an unnecessary and time-wasting exercise that fails to create value and just represents another layer of bureaucracy. Whilst it is impossible to say that this is never the case, properly designed and executed by the directors, corporate governance measures should enhance rather than hinder company leadership and thus, performance.

Changes in governance at CFI might not be needed as long as Howard intends to retain full ownership of the company. However, given that he is considering an exit strategy in the not too distant future, some improvements in governance are probably warranted to demonstrate to potential buyers that the company is effectively governed and managed.

(c) Issuing performance linked shares to senior staff could be a good idea provided that the motivational impact of this initiative helped create/maintain congruence between the senior staff and the existing shareholders (currently Howard and his immediate family).

The staff involved would need to be convinced that the proposal would be of benefit. Explaining this to the senior staff could be difficult and might make it necessary to advise the staff of the intention to sell the business.

Howard’s wife and sons may or may not respond positively to the idea as it would dilute their proportionate ownership of CFI.
