## Presentation skill
[https://1drv.ms/b/s!AihkWrdX8YEwgpZccu8BN4PsQOGocg?e=sVCX6I](https://1drv.ms/b/s!AihkWrdX8YEwgpZccu8BN4PsQOGocg?e=sVCX6I)
Impactful presentation 
1. Imagine your audience (background, hope, concern)
	- ask yourself: “who are your audiences and what are they looking for from your presentation?”
	- looking at one’s background, hopes & concerns
2. Stick to the basics (structuring -\> focus and interest)
	- open: catchy
	- Body: key points
	- Close: summarise
3. Keep slides few and simple
	- KISS (short and simple), KILL (large and legible)
	- visual aids - support; express; you
4. Be ready for questions  (keep control)
	- difficult questions: no answers, irrelevant, argumentative
5. Speak with confidence (topic, voice, body language)


### Imagine your audience
- background
	- who will be attending the presentation?
	- what’s their level of seniority/ importance?
	- will people be attending your presentation by choice?
- Hopes
	- what will they be expecting to hear from you?
	- what sort of concerns will be the most relevant to their work?
- concerns
	- what they fear the most on your subject?
	- what outcomes will then not prefer to see at their work?
### Stick to the basics
- opening (tell them what you are going to tell them)
	- can include humour, story, question, short video, quotation, some statistics, recent news
	- relevant to the “hopes” and “concerns” of your audience
	- Method: INTRO
- Body
	- list up to 3 key points for your presentation (hopes and concerns)
	- key points require support and research to back them up
- Close
	- summarise your key points and messages
	- ask audience if they have any questions before proceeding to the next presentation
### Keep slides few and simple
1. Visual aids whole only support the ‘must haves’ of a presentation
2. Avoid showing continual PowerPoint slides. Black the scree and give a verbal commentary
3. Visual aids should express and not impress!
4. You are the most important visual aid!
### Be ready for question
1. Pause before you answer a question - it shows the audience you are carefully thinking
2. Appreciate the audience’s question before attempting it
3. Don’t forget vocal delivery and body language when answering questions
4. Take one step back when someone asks a question - this forces them to speak louder enabling everyone to hear
5. Predict likely questions and plan the answers
6. Ask for questions and wait - pausing can encourage people to speak up
7. Address answers to the whole audience
8. Keep control!
### Speak with confidence
1. adopt a delivery style that is comfortable for you, don’t try and be somebody else
2. when writing your speech underline the key points and then decide what body language, verbal delivery and facial expressions you can use to support those key points and then practice your delivery
3. show the audience you have enthusiasm and believe in the topic and you are happy to be presenting it.
4. your presentation voice is different from your social voice - slow down, vary your volume, speak a little louder stress key points
5. don’t speak too softly - a louder voice can show confidence
6. It’s okay to be nervous but don’t tell the audience you are nervous or that it is your first presentation
7. Maintain eye contact with everyone in the audience
8. Pause after each key point to stress the important point
9. For a very important point - say it, pause, and then say it again.
10. Tell the audience if you are about to say something important - ‘my next point is extremely important’
11. You must practice your presentation!

## Tips to make impactful presentations
[https://1drv.ms/b/s!AihkWrdX8YEwgpZeL3nMcm6rglNz6w?e=qPCn16](https://1drv.ms/b/s!AihkWrdX8YEwgpZeL3nMcm6rglNz6w?e=qPCn16)
1. Prepare beforehand
	- Be clear about the purpose of your presentation.
	- Focus on your audience's needs and objectives.
	- Set out the key messages that your audience needs to take away.
	- Get there early and test your equipment well in advance.
2. Controlling nerves
	- Take a deep breath and release it slowly repeat this.
	- Stretch your body. 
	- Stay positive and keep smiling.
	- Have some casual chats with the audience before presentation.
	- Drink warm water before presentation.
3. Speak confidently
	- Your voice should project to the farthest audience in the room. 
	- Pause briefly each time you make an important point.
	- You body language is the best visual aids.
4. Close effectively
	- Three or four main things for take away is probably enough.
	- Do not rush off pause between the summary and the Q&A.
	- Be concise. Don't put anything new in the conclusion.
5. Dealing with questions 
	- Anticipate likely questions and plan ahead (e.g. what are the audience's concerns).
	- Take the heat out of an aggressive question by acknowledging first.
	- Try to avoid a confrontation by acknowledging the concern behind the questions and then making positive suggestions for handling the problem.

## 5 tips to better business writing
1. Write with a purpose - clear purpose -\> write easier and faster
2. Choose simple words - simplify -\> sound friendly and natural
3. Be specific - precision (help you imagine)
4. Stay active - subject first (active voice)
5. 2nd draft = 1st draft - 10% - concise (cut unnecessary words)

# Risk management
[https://1drv.ms/b/s!AihkWrdX8YEwgpZjM6UoKs6OsnMSpw?e=Jb6TgP](https://1drv.ms/b/s!AihkWrdX8YEwgpZjM6UoKs6OsnMSpw?e=Jb6TgP)

In Chapter 9 of Module 12, the principles of risk management are discussed comprehensively. The following sections provide a quick summary and additional discussion on risk management.

## What is risk and what is not?
![](Paper.QP_Final.20.png)![](Risk%20management.png)
### 1.1 understanding risk 
Risk is an  **effect** of uncertainty on objectives  (ISO 31000: 2018) whereas **uncertainty** relates to circumstances in which the **full set of potential outcomes** in a given situation are not known. In other words, risk is an uncertainty that if it occurs could **affect one or more objectives**. It is important to link risks with objectives because it will ensure that the risk identification process focuses on those **uncertainties that matter**, rather than being distracted and diverted by irrelevant uncertainties (Hillson D.A.)

The link between risks and objectives is critical in identifying risks at different levels, based on the hierarchy of objectives in an organisation. For example, **strategic risks** are uncertainties affecting strategic objectives whereas **financial risks** affect financial objectives.

On the other hand, risks must be distinguished from causes and effects of risks. Causes are definite events or facts that give rise to uncertainty and as such they are not uncertain. If causes are mistakenly labeled as “risks”, risk assessment process will be distorted because genuine risks may consequently not receive appropriate degree of attention.

Effects are **unplanned variation** which arise as a result of **risks** occurring. In other words, they are **contingent events** that will not happen unless risks occur. The purpose of risk management is to manage genuine risks. If risk management is effective, the  shall not happen. As such, effects cannot be managed through the risk management process because they may not yet exist and even never exist.

Risk assessment consists of two dimensions to measure risk, namely “probability of occurrence” and “impact”. Only genuine risks have both of these dimensions. There is no associated “probability” of causes because they are definite and certain whereas effects are simply a description of the “impact” dimension of a risk.

### 1.2 categories of risk
While there are many ways to categorise risks, the four main categories are strategic risks, reputation risk, financial risks and operational risks.

#### 1.2.1 Strategic risks 
Strategic risk is the **possibility** that unexpected or uncertain events will affect a company's **strategy**, creating downside or upside implications for profitability and the company's long term interests.

**Factors** that determine the **nature and significance** of strategic risks include the:
- Types of industries/markets within which the business operates 
- State of the economy 
- Actions of competitors and the possibility of mergers and acquisitions 
- Stage in a product's life cycle, higher risks in the introductory and declining stages 
- Dependence on inputs with fluctuating prices, such as the prices of wheat, rice, oil 
- Company's operating gearing – the proportion of fixed costs to total costs – which affects changes in profitability given an increase or fall in sales revenue
- Flexibility of production processes to adapt to different specifications or products 
- Organisation's research and development capacity and ability to innovate 
- Significance of new technology
Some **examples** of sources of strategic risks:
- environment: risks arising from the impact of the organisation on the natural environment or the natural environment on the organisation. They include risks from shortages of natural resources, global warming and rising sea levels, from levels of pollution or wastage, and from overconsumption of scarce natural resources. 
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-49.png)
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-50.png)
#### 1.2.2 Reputation risk
Strategic risks may arise from factors that affect the company's reputation. Reputation and the ongoing ethical behaviour of a company are increasingly important.

When the trust of customers and regulators in a company is damaged, it takes a long time for customers to believe in the company and products again. Damage to reputation may also result in additional regulation of the industry.

#### 1.2.3 Financial risks 
Companies face a variety of financial risks:
- Risks of fraud and misuse of their financial resources.
- Credit risk: the possibility of payment default by the customer.
- Liquidity risk: the risk of being unable to finance the credit, arising from cash restrictions or the need for more cash.
- Cash management risk: risks relating to the security of cash, risks arising from unpredictable cash flows.
- Interest rate risk: future interest rates cannot be easily predicted. If a firm has a significant amount of variable (floating) rate debt, interest rate movements will give rise to uncertainty about the cost of servicing this debt. Conversely, if a company uses a lot of fixed rate debt, it will lose out if interest rates begin to fall. There are arrangements and financial products that a treasury department can use to reduce exposure to interest rate risk, such as interest rate swaps.
- Currency risks: risks of loss (or gain) due to future changes in exchange rates. There are ways of reducing or eliminating this risk, for example by the use of forward exchange contracts.
- Market risks: these are risks of loss (or gain) due to an adverse move in the market value of an asset – a stock, a bond, a loan, foreign exchange or a commodity – or a derivative contract linked to these assets.

#### 1.2.4 Operational risks 
Operational risk is the risk of loss from a failure of internal business and control processes. They are risks from what could go wrong on a day to day basis, and are not generally relevant to strategic risk or the key strategic decisions that affect a business.

## Steps in risk management
### 2.1 Approaches to risk management
Risk management is a continuous process of coordinated activities to direct and control an organisation with regard to risk (ISO 31000:2018). The purpose of risk management is to create and protect value of an organisation by improving performance, encouraging innovation and supporting the achievement of objectives.

Companies and their boards of directors will view and react to the risks they face in different ways. How an organisation approaches risk will depend on the risk appetite, risk attitude and capacity to bear risk.
- Risk appetite is the nature and extent of risk that a company's board of directors is willing to accept.
- Risk attitude is the directors' views about the level of risk that they consider desirable. Risk attitude may be quantified, in terms of risk tolerance limits.
- Risk capacity is the maximum amount of risk the organisation can safely bear without putting itself and its future existence at risk.
 ![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-51.png) 
### 2.2 Key steps in risk management
The risk management process involves a systematic application of policies, procedures and practices to a series of activities in dealing with risk. These activities include risk assessment, risk treatment, monitoring and review, recording and reporting, and communication and consultation.

#### Risk management process and Actions involved
##### Risk assessment
- Risk identification
- Risk analysis
- Risk evaluation
##### Risk treatment
- Select risk treatment options
- Implement risk treatment plans
##### Monitoring and review
- Ongoing monitoring and periodic review of the risk management process and its outcomes
##### Recording and reporting
- Document risk management process
- Report to top management and oversight bodies
##### Communication and consultation
- Inform relevant stakeholders in understanding risk
- Obtain feedback to support decision-making
Reference can also be made to Section 9.1.1 of Module 12.
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-52.png)

## Illustrative examples
### 1. Risk management methods 
Telecentres Ltd operates a number of data centres in a number of cities in South-East Asia. A data centre houses computer systems and associated components, such as telecommunications and storage systems. It provides a secure and highly connected environment for IT and telecommunications equipment. Separate data networks that make up the internet meet and are connected at each centre, providing users of the centre with connectivity. Data centres also host bandwidth-intensive applications and internet content on behalf of client companies.

An essential requirement for a data centre is that the IT systems of client organisations **must never experience an operating failure**. Each centre therefore includes redundant (backup) power supplies, and redundant data communications connections, as well as environmental controls (such as air conditioning and fire suppression systems) and security devices.

Some companies, such as major telecommunications companies, operate their own data centres. Telecentres Ltd, however, operates data centres for client organisations such as internet content providers that **do not have their own centres.** Many customers are content providers (such as companies providing games, music and videos over the internet). Customers also include financial services organisations, as well as telecommunications operators that use data centres to connect to internet exchanges, each other and internet content companies.

Data centres also offer providers of cloud computing services a means of connecting to customers through private networks, rather than over the public internet.

#### Data centre fundamentals 
Data centres must have **access to high-capacity power supplies**. They have Uninterruptible Power Supply – systems and standby diesel generators capable of supporting the site indefinitely in the event of a failure of power from the electricity grid. Data centres also provides an appropriate environment for IT hardware to operate effectively (such as cooling and humidity control systems), as well as high levels of security and fire protection.

Centres are supported by engineering teams, on site 24 hours a day and 7 days a week, to manage the facility and provide customer support. Customer equipment is connected to networks by means of physical cable connections, which are installed and managed by the company's own engineers.

#### Risks 
The company has identified and rated its main risks as follows. (The numbers are for identification purposes only. They do not indicate risk priorities.)
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-53.png)
* Building an efficient facility at a data centre **depends on knowing what future customer requirements will be**. There is a risk of making incorrect forecasts of customer demand.

Although not given in the question, it is also worthwhile to seek for more information to identify risks relevant to the situations in South-East Asia regarding natural environment (e.g. earthquake, flooding), political environment (e.g. civil war, local rebellion), and legal environment (e.g. corruption, data protection, cyber security).

For the risks identified above, the suitable measures for managing each of these risks can be summarized as follow:
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-54.png)
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-55.png)
**Note on insurance**: Insurance is a method of managing risk, by transferring it to an insurance organisation or insurance arrangement. However, the cost of insuring a risk can also be used as a financial measure of the risk. Delegates may therefore discuss insurance and risk transfer as part of their response to the case study exercise, for example insurance against accidents at work.

### 2. Risk identification and assessment
Risk identification and assessment Fruit Juice Ltd, founded in 2003, is a listed company that manufactures and supplies fruit juice drinks with about 1000 employees. Its mission statement is:

> To create and maintain a sustainable company that embraces product innovation to satisfy customer needs for healthier drinks while maintaining community trust.

Recently the Marketing Department identified an** increasing trend in requests from its customers** for a line of plant-based juices. After looking at the opportunity, and considering the risks, the company decided to produce a line of plant-based juice drinks. Based on its historical performance, the company set a target of the new line representing 20% of their production by the end of the first year.

The following cascading objectives (Figure 1) within the company were set by senior management to meet this new target.
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-56.png)
Each of these departments then, separately, discussed the risks associated with these objectives at their own level and then selected their own approach, based on initial views of their new goals. 
- The Procurement Department had an initial view that many risks would be **similar** to those relating to procurement of fruit juice, and selected an approach based on round-table discussions.
- The Manufacturing Department also had an initial view that many risks would be **similar** to those relating to its existing fruit juice production. After initial discussions, they soon realized that **adding a new product line introduced greater complexity to scheduling**.

	Consequently, they developed more detailed modelling to better understand the risks of introducing this new product line.

- The Distribution Department wanted to take a different approach from the start. They undertook a **process analysis **to better understand what risks could impact their existing distribution channels and what the impacts might be.

- The Marketing Department felt that they needed more external information. They used a variety of publicly available data to capture risks, and they ran a series of **focus groups** with potential users to understand the possible risks that may impact their ability to generate sales.

A risk worksheet is prepared by each Department in order to assess the risks with the severity of each risk color-coded: red = high; orange = medium; and green = low.
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-57.png)![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-58.png)
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-59.png)![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-60.png)
#### Risk Aggregation 
Once all the departments identified and assessed the risks associated with the relevant objectives, senior management aggregated the information at the enterprise level. That information helps them to better understand how the likelihood and impact of the risks may change at different levels of the company.

The consolidation is shown in Figure 2 with the severity of each risk color-coded: red = high; orange = medium; and green = low.

To assess the severity of risk on enterprise objectives, they used information from a review of business plans and budgets; prior risk assessments; financial, board, and annual reports; customer surveys; and social media postings. In addition, they used the company's historical risk occurrence and publicly available information from other small beverage companies to determine the likelihood of the risk occurring.
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-61.png)
In addition to an analysis performed for each department, the severity of risks is further assessed at four different levels.

1. Risks that could significantly impact a single objective Two high risks are identified on a single objective based on the score of “Likelihood” times “Impact”.

	Manufacturing objective: meet customer demand Risk: “The possibility of poor product quality or consistency and impact on meeting customer needs.” (Likelihood: 4 x Impact: 5 = 20)

	Distribution objective: get product into distribution channels used by customers Risk: “The possibility that the new product line cannot be placed at current vendors and the impact on inventory” (Likelihood: 4 x Impact: 4 = 16)

2. Common risks across multiple objectives and hence could be considered significant as a result 
	(a) One similar risk (marked as “A” on Figure 2) was noted by two different groups: marketing and distribution. Each group also assesses this risk differently.
	(b) Three risks relate to possibly higher costs being experienced by Friendly Juice Ltd. (marked as “B” on Figure 2)
	 - B1 – “Shift to plant-based juices would result in higher costs” 
	- B2 – “Cost of manufacturing plant based juices is higher” 
	- B3 – “Unfavourable changes in pricing”

	Each department notes that the above risks could impact the achievement of financial goals, but within each department, each risk was still within the company’s risk appetite. However, the collective impact on the overall business objective should be assessed.

3. Objectives that have a greater number of risks 
	Five objectives have more than one risk identified:
	e.g. “Produce high quality juices at best possible price”: 2 medium risks “Generate new sales for plant-based juice line”: 3 risks, 1 high, one medium and one low.

4. Dependencies between different risks and objectives that could influence their rating There can be dependency between two objectives, one relating to procurement and one relating to manufacturing (marked as “C” on Figure 2). The pricing (cost) risks to one of those objectives may impact the ability to achieve the other and the overall business objective.

## Practice Questions
### 1. COSO ERM Framework and completing a risk record
Lee Trading Limited is an energy trading company founded by David Lee and family. The company is owned partly by the Lee family, and partly by a small number of private companies and venture capitalists. It is now a listed company on the Hong Kong Stock Exchange.

Lee Trading's core business is the trading and supply of crude oil. Lee purchases the crude oil from producers, transports it, stores it, processes it when needed and then delivers it around the world. They trade mainly in the Asian market but also have high trading volume in North and South America, Russia, the Middle East and North and West Africa.

Crude oil is transported via ship (tanker). Lee is proud of the relationship the company has with its shipping suppliers and ensures that ships meet international environmental, safety and shipping standards. They conduct regular reviews and audits as required under law of all suppliers' ships.

Lee Trading is governed by a board of directors. A board member, Nadir Elhage, is also one of the owners of Crude Oil Shipping Ltd. Lee Trading uses Crude Oil Shipping for most of its Middle Eastern shipping.

On the recommendation of the Financial Controller, the finance team has recently run some systems reporting on the financial consequences of various percentage losses and leakage of cargo, as well as detailed quantified risk reporting. The finance team of Lee Trading did not address any sustainability or environmental factors in its reporting or any qualitative measures.

As a consequence of this reporting, the logistics team has conducted spot inspections on supplier tankers. They recently inspected one of Crude Oil's tankers and found that there was very high potential for small loss and leakage of cargo into the ocean due to faulty maintenance. The Chair of Lee Trading, a family friend of Nadir's, had a quiet word with him about it and asked him to address the issue before they reported it.

A week later, Lee requested they inspect the rest of the tanker fleet. Nadir Elhage refused the request. He then emailed the Board Chair and the CEO of Lee to report that the maintenance issue had been fixed on the inspected tanker and that it was not an issue on any other ships. He asked them to 'drop the matter – they had his word it was fixed'.

Required:
(i) Classify the risk in this case study into the relevant category or categories: financial control; operational efficiency; reputational risk; risks in commercial activities and complete a record of the risk.
(ii) Suggest two relevant benchmarks that could be used to assess the risk in this situation.

Suggested answer

(i) Risk classification 
The risk here could be classified as a reputational risk and an operational risk.

Risk record
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-62.png)
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-63.png)
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-64.png)
(ii) Benchmark as an operational efficiency risk:

A suitable benchmark to apply to operational efficiency risks may be the length of the disruption. For example, if the issue disrupts one supplier ship but another can be replaced then the risk is Low. If the issue means the entire fleet of supplier ships is out of action for a period of time and new suppliers have to be found, the risk is Medium. If the issue means the entire fleet is affected and the supplier is put out of business, then the risk is High.

Benchmark as a reputational risk:

The supplier ships are leaking crude oil into the ocean. A suitable benchmark could be the expected impact on Lee's share price. If it results in no drop or a steady drop in the share price, then the risk is Low. If it results in a drop of 0–10% then the risk is Medium. If the drop is over 10% then the risk is High. A timescale for share price drop could also be applied.

### 2. Risk identification and treatment
Renaissance International Fashion Ltd (“Renaissance”) is a public listed firm based in Tokyo, Japan; its management is considering a new nine-year expansion project that will develop 390 smart-fashion stores in mainland China and Hong Kong. To the management team, it will be a long term strategy, as the middle class in mainland China is increasing at a high rate. Their relative high disposable income will create enough demand for Renaissance’s products. The smart-fashion stores are equipped with Renaissance’s retail artificial intelligence (“AI”) technology, such as endless aisles, mobile check-in, customisation kiosks and smart mirrors.

The smart-fashion store investment is somewhat riskier than the traditional fashion store project that Renaissance currently undertakes, so its management uses the subjective approach and applies an additional adjustment factor of 3% to the cost of capital for this smart-fashion store project.

With the economic slowdown in mainland China due to lower export growth (trade war with the United States), recent news reported that the Chinese government will continue its prudent monetary policy, flexibly use monetary instruments and maintain moderate liquidity to achieve reasonable growth in loans and social financing. The Chinese government has been fairly accommodative to control the amount of money supply to the economy in order to keep small and medium sized enterprises to obtain easier credit. Moreover, the Chinese government will also encourage foreign enterprises to continue investing in mainland China in order to create more employment opportunities.

In line with the expansion plan, Renaissance decided to buy three buildings (“the buildings”) last year for RMB3.9 billion in anticipation of using them as warehouses in three major cities in mainland China for the smart-fashion store project. At the time of purchase, the exchange rate was JPY19.3/RMB. In eight years, when the project is expected to finish, the buildings’ market value will be RMB3.3 billion according to the latest leading surveyor’s estimation due to oversupply of similar buildings in mainland China. In another forecast, the exchange rate between the two currencies is expected to reach JPY14/RMB in eight years amid the slowing economic growth in mainland China.

John Chan, the Chief Financial Officer (“CFO”) of Renaissance and Hong Kong certified public accountant, recently suggested to sell the buildings bought last year. Instead, the firm should go for leasing space in mainland China in view of the possible downturn in the real estate market in mainland China even though Renaissance has strong cash flow to go for the buy decision.

Another concern relates to John’s feeling that the good run for the past ten years in real estate in different sectors within mainland China may not be sustainable. A correction in pricing may be coming soon. John would like to speed up the expansion project in mainland China so that he can rent fashion stores in first tier cities in mainland China quickly.

Required:

(i) Evaluate the financial and non-financial risks faced by Renaissance when it purchased the buildings for expansion.

(ii) Recommend methods Renaissance could use to hedge the financial risks as described in Question (a).

(iii) The CFO suggests that leasing space in mainland China would make more sense to Renaissance instead of buying the buildings. Justify the CFO’s suggestion.
---- 
#### Suggested answer

##### Answer (i) 
Financial risks:

Asset valuations and pricings may fluctuate significantly due to changes of macro-economic factors in mainland China.

Exchange rate is highly unpredictable. The depreciation of RMB against JPY in the nine-year period could add on the loss to Renaissance.

Economic growth is a challenging issue in mainland China. It might affect the operating environment of Renaissance to pursue a long-term business venture.

Non-financial risks:

Intelligent smart fashion stores utilises updated information system and technologies that poses an information system risk. Renaissance has to upgrade information system and infrastructure frequently and timely to continue their smart fashion stores’ businesses and operations.

Renaissance has cybersecurity risk that should be handled professionally and diligently with cybersecurity professionals to prevent hacker attack and loss of business data and information.

Political and legal risks are high due to different political systems and legal practices in mainland China and Japan that should be considered and handled proactively.

Renaissance has to handle the operating risk of changing from operating traditional fashion stores to smart fashion stores.

##### Answer (ii) 
Renaissance may consider to diversify the building portfolios in different cities in mainland China. It will lower the concentration risk of the assets to the firm.

Renaissance could finance the purchase of buildings by borrowing RMB. It helps to mitigate the loss of declining value of RMB and simultaneously reduce the JPY cost of the liability to finance the assets. Alternatively, Renaissance could take out a long-dated over the counter forward contract to hedge the JPY value of the buildings with a Japanese financial institution. It could be tailored according to the foreign exchange exposure to the firm.

##### Answer (iii) 
Lease payments are tax deductible that provides additional tax benefits to Renaissance in mainland China. Renaissance can use the downpayment to invest in other projects that generate additional returns to the shareholders.

Renaissance can enjoy flexibility to change their business development plans in mainland China easily if they lease stores.

Renaissance can avoid a significant amount of borrowing in RMB that poses significant foreign exchange risk when they have to pay interest and repay the loan in RMB.

Leasing will largely reduce the exposure of real estate price risk of Renaissance in mainland China.

The transaction costs of leasing are likely to be lower than that of buying the buildings and financing them with debt or equity.

The sale proceeds from the disposal of properties can be used for other financing purposes.

### 3. Risk and strategy 
Shine Stars Holdings Inc (“SSHI” or the “Company”) was incorporated in Hong Kong and the Company is planning to be listed on the Hong Kong Stock Exchange. SSHI and its subsidiaries (the “Group”) are controlled and 70% owned by the Shine Family. The Group has a financial year end of 31 December and the financial statements are prepared in accordance with the Hong Kong Financial Reporting Standards. The Group is subject to Hong Kong profit tax at 16.5%.

The Group has been engaging in selling health care products, such as Men’s and Women’s Nutrition, Vitamins and Minerals, Joint Support and Nutritional Oil and Juice etc. for more than 15 years and is the leader in the Hong Kong market. It is well-known for its Vitamins and Minerals products and many tourists from mainland China purchase these products when they visit Hong Kong.

Andy Shine is the founder of the Group. He is the Chairman of the Board, Executive Director, and is a member of the Nomination Committee and Remuneration Committee of the Group. Andy is responsible for the strategic direction and overall performance of the Group. The Board also consists of two other Independent Non-Executive Directors, Tom Wong and Peter Lee. Tom is also a member of the Audit Committee and Nomination Committee of the Group. Peter is also the Chairman of the Nomination Committee and a member of the Remuneration Committee of the Group.

The Management Discussion and Analysis Report The Group, as a socially responsible company, focuses heavily on customers’ needs and has developed various products two years ago, such as the Joint Support supplements, which fit customers’ needs.

On the Management Discussion and Analysis Report attached with the draft group financial statements, the management provides the information about customer demographics, products offered as well as the sales and profit margin etc. The Vitamins and Minerals products are always the best-selling products of the Group. However, the management particularly stressed that the Joint Support supplements are the best-selling products due to the aging problem and the Sales and Promotion Team will focus on these products in the coming years.

Expansion Plan In one of the recent meetings, the management discussed an expansion plan in Hong Kong and the overseas market.

For the expansion plan in Hong Kong, the management discussed and met with the following parties:

The management met with the managers of supermarkets and pharmacy stores requesting favourable product placement (e.g. eye level position, near the entrance) for three years with the total fee at HK$2 million (“Slotting fee”). The Slotting fee is non-refundable and upon the supermarkets and pharmacy stores placing the first sales order with the Group, payment of the Slotting fee will be made. The supermarkets and pharmacy stores will not be committed to any minimum purchases quantities in the future upon the payment of the Slotting fee. The Finance Manager proposed that the slotting fee is a one off prepaid item; and the Head of the Sales and Promotion Team proposed to the managers of supermarkets and pharmacy stores that the exchange of products is not permitted.

The management also met with the distributors and discussed the consignment arrangement. The management understands that the design and renovation of each store is different due to unique specifications; therefore, the Group will sponsor the distributors for a design and renovation fee. In order to be entitled to the sponsorship of the design and renovation fee, the distributors are required to commit to a minimum sales quantity for the coming three years. If the distributors fail to meet the minimum quantity, the distributors are required to make a refund of the sponsorship of the design and renovation fee to the Group on a pro rata basis.

Besides, the Group will provide training to the distributors about the ingredients and functions of the products as well as training on how to sell the products of the Group. The management and the distributors agreed that exchange of products is allowed but a refund is not permitted.

For the expansion plan outside Hong Kong, the management planned to raise capital in Hong Kong by various means, such as requesting a bank loan from a bank, issuing debts, preference shares and ordinary shares. The Group considered many factors in several countries for possible expansion, such as, labour cost and foreign currency fluctuation etc. and the Group has identified that the United Kingdom (“UK”) is the optimal market for overseas expansion.

Required:

SSHI business is subject to numerous business and operation risks that may have a significant and adverse impact. With the case background information, identify the risk factors related to business and operations, and formulate strategies to mitigate the identified risks.
---- 
#### Suggested answer

SSHI business is subject to numerous risks that may have a significant and adverse impact. Below are the risk factors and strategies to mitigate the identified risks.

#### Risk factor 1:
The SSHI business can be adversely affected by the change of customer choice, customer preference or spending habits. SSHI is well-known for its Vitamins and Minerals products and many tourists from mainland China purchase those products when they visit Hong Kong; however, if the customer preference or spending habits switch, then it could have an adverse impact on the business.

##### Strategy to mitigate the risk factor:
SSHI can enrich the Vitamins and Minerals products or develop new products that meet the customer preference.

#### Risk factor 2:
The management of SSHI understands that the Joint Support supplements are the best selling products in the Hong Kong market due to the aging problem and SSHI will focus on those products in the coming years; however, SSHI business can be adversely affected if the distribution agreement is terminated.

##### Strategy to mitigate the risk factor:
SSHI can explore if the distribution agreement can be extended to ensure the suppliers that there is an enough supply of the Joint Support supplements products in the coming years so that the Sales and Promotion Team can continue to focus on those products without affecting the operation of the business.

#### Risk factor 3:
SSHI incurs a lot of Slotting fees and the sponsorship fees to the design and renovation of the consignment stores. However, if the supermarkets and consignment stores change their business or pricing strategies, then the Hong Kong expansion plan of SSHI can be adversely affected.

##### Strategy to mitigate the risk factor:
SSHI can discuss with the supermarkets and consignment stores about the quantities and price for the coming years to ensure that the expansion plan will not be affected.

#### Risk factor 4:
SSHI relies heavily on the sales through the supermarkets and consignment stores and SSHI does not have much control over the supermarkets and consignment stores.

##### Strategy to mitigate the risk factor:
SSHI can find more sub-distributors to sell the products or explore the possibility for e-commerce business.

#### Risk factor 5:
SSHI will provide training to the distributors about the ingredients and functions of the products. SSHI may be exposed to litigation claims if the distributors can not explain the ingredients and functions of the products to the customers clearly and so cause health problems.

##### Strategy to mitigate the risk factor:
SSHI can purchase an insurance to cover or partially cover the claims.

#### Risk factor 6:
The Customer Relationship Management (CRM) System may be hacked and SSHI can lose all the data.

##### Strategy to mitigate the risk factor:
SSHI can maintain and secure the CRM System by recruiting IT experts to maintain the IT security of the CRM System. SSHI can also back up all the data off site as a disaster recovery plan. In case, the CRM System is down, the back up off site can ensure the System and servers can be reloaded with the latest data.

#### Risk factor 7:
The fluctuation of the pound currency (i.e. the new investment) against the Hong Kong dollar can affect SSHI’s business and its profitability.

##### Strategy to mitigate the risk factor:
SSHI can manage the foreign currency fluctuation risk through currency futures, forwards, options, and currency-focused exchange-traded funds etc.

#### Risk factor 8:
SSHI increases the long-term bank loan by 10 million and the fluctuation of the interest rate can affect SSHI’s business and its profitability.

##### Strategy to mitigate the risk factor:
SSHI can manage the interest rate risk through entering the interest rate futures and purchasing the high-yield bonds.

#### Risk factor 9:
SSHI may not receive the outstanding payments from its debtors and this credit risk can affect SSHI’s business and its profitability.
##### Strategy to mitigate the risk factor:
SSHI can discuss the terms of repayment with its debtors in order to manage the credit risk.

## Internal control and risk management 
- [a basic framework (HKICPA)](https://1drv.ms/b/s!AihkWrdX8YEwgpZhVfQgV_wGTRxMUg?e=Hza62K)
## A. OBJECTIVES
### 1.0 Background
1.1 The Stock Exchange of Hong Kong Limited (“Stock Exchange”) published the Code on Corporate Governance Practices (“the Code”) and Corporate Governance Report in November 2004. These were subsequently incorporated into Appendices 14 and 23 of the Main Board Listing Rules and Appendices 15 and 16 of the Growth Enterprise Market (“GEM”) Listing Rules respectively. The Code, with one exception, became effective for accounting periods commencing on or after 1 January 2005. The exception is in respect of Code provision C.2 on internal controls and the proposed disclosure requirements in the Corporate Governance Report relating to listed issuers’ internal controls, which take effect for accounting periods commencing on or after 1 July 2005.

1.2 The Stock Exchange invited the Hong Kong Institute of Certified Public Accountants (“the Institute”) to issue further guidance to help listed issuers understand and implement the Code requirements relating to internal control and devise their internal control procedures.

1.3 The Institute agreed to take up the Stock Exchange’s invitation. A task force, set up under the Corporate Governance Committee and including representatives from the Auditing and Assurance Standards Committee and the Professional Accountants in Business Committee, was formed to undertake the project

### 2.0 Listing Rule requirements on internal control
2.1 Principle C.2 of the Code states that: “The board should ensure that the issuer maintains _sound and effective internal controls_ to safeguard the shareholders’ investment and the issuer’s assets.”

2.2 Code provision C.2.1 on “Internal Controls” states that: “The directors should at least annually conduct a review of the effectiveness of the system of internal control of the issuer and its subsidiaries and report to shareholders that they have done so in their Corporate Governance Report. The review should cover all material controls, including financial, operational and compliance controls and risk management functions.”

2.3 The recommended best practices in relation to reviewing internal controls and the related disclosures are set out in C.2.2 to C.2.5 of the Code. Listed companies are encouraged to adopt the recommended best practices.

2.4 The note to paragraph 2 of Appendix 23 (Main Board Listing Rules) and Appendix 16 (GEM Listing Rules), which sets out the specific disclosures pertaining to the Code provisions that a listed issuer is expected to make in its Corporate Governance Report, contains the following disclosure in relation to the Code provision on “Internal Controls”:
- “(3) a statement that the board has conducted a review of the effectiveness of the system of internal control of the issuer and its subsidiaries (C.2.1 of the Code).”
2.5 Where a listed issuer includes a statement on the review of its system of internal control in the annual report, pursuant to provision C.2.1 of the Code, it is encouraged to disclose the details set out in paragraph 3(d) of Appendix 23 of the Main Board Listing Rules and Appendix 16 of the GEM Listing Rules, as appropriate.
( A fuller extract of the relevant Code Provisions and Recommended Best Practices and the Corporate Governance Report required and recommended disclosures can be found at Appendix I.)

### 3.0 Objectives of the guide
3.1 The primary objective of this guide is to provide general guidance and recommendations on a basic framework of internal control. This should help listed issuers understand and implement the requirements in the Code relating to internal control, and to devise their own internal control procedures that take account of the particular circumstances and characteristics of their own business and operation. The guide is not intended to be exhaustive or prescriptive, but should nevertheless be useful to directors, managers and other personnel that are accountable for control in a company.

3.2 It is also intended to:
(i) help improve understanding of the conceptual framework of internal control and risk management;
(ii) help provide a framework/basis that can be used to develop and assess the effectiveness of internal control in a company; and
(iii) reflect sound business practice whereby internal control is embedded in the business and management processes by which a company pursues its objectives.

3.3 The Stock Exchange indicated that in preparing the Code, it had, in particular, taken into account the principles and guidelines set out in the revised Combined Code on Corporate Governance (“the Combined Code”) issued by the Financial Reporting Council in the United Kingdom (“UK”) in July 2003. The Preamble to the Combined Code makes reference to specific guidance on how to comply with particular parts of the Combined Code. Internal Control: Guidance for Directors on the Combined Code (“the Turnbull Guidance”)1 is the guidance relevant to the provisions on internal control. In preparing this guide, the Institute has referred to the Turnbull Guidance.

3.4 The Institute considers that the report, Internal Control – Integrated Framework, issued by the Committee of Sponsoring Organizations of the Treadway Commission (“COSO”) in the United States, in 1992, contains a definition of internal control and a conceptual framework that are constructive and relevant. Where appropriate, therefore, this guide adopts the approach outlined in the COSO report.

3.5 Boards of listed companies are encouraged to make reference to this guide in:
• assessing how the company has applied Code principle C.2;
• implementing the requirements of Code provision C.2.1; and
• reporting on these matters to shareholders in the Corporate Governance Report.

3.6 Directors are expected to exercise judgement in reviewing how the company has implemented the requirements of the Code relating to internal control and reporting to shareholders thereon.

3.7 The guidance set out herein in relation to establishing a sound system of internal control and reviewing its effectiveness should be incorporated by the company within its normal management and governance processes, from a corporate governance point of view, as part of the accountability of a company’s board and management to shareholders, and should not be treated as a separate exercise undertaken to meet regulatory requirements issued and enforced by a securities market regulator.

### 4.0 Applicability of the guide

4.1 This guide is aimed primarily at listed companies and their subsidiaries, to which Code provision C.2.1 applies. However, listed companies are very diverse in nature. Internal controls should be tailored to an individual company’s own particular characteristics and circumstances, which may depend upon, for example, its industry, size and organisational structure. Accordingly, it is not appropriate to adopt a “one size fits all” approach.

4.2 It is believed that the principles and recommendations contained in this guide will provide a useful reference for most listed companies, although they may need to be adapted according to the circumstances of the company concerned. All companies that are part of a listed group are encouraged to take on board these principles and recommendations, and it is hoped that companies in general that wish to implement or enhance their system of internal control will find this guide to be a useful reference.

4.3 Throughout the guide, where reference is made to “company”, it should be taken, where applicable, as referring to the group of which the reporting company is the parent company. For groups of companies, the review of the effectiveness of internal control and the report to the shareholders should be from the perspective of the group as a whole, e.g., groups of companies should review the effectiveness of all significant controls at all significant locations.

4.4 Where material joint ventures and associates have not been dealt with as part of the group for the purposes of applying this guidance, companies are encouraged to disclose this. Where they exist, alternative sources of risk management and internal control assurance applied to these entities should also be disclosed.

## B. IMPLEMENTING INTERNAL CONTROL AND RISK MANAGEMENT
### 1.0 Framework and scope of internal control

1.1 There is no simple definition of “internal control”. However, as indicated in paragraph A.3.4 above, where appropriate, this guide adopts the definition and conceptual framework described in the COSO report, which the Institute regards as a useful model. (See also Appendix II).

1.2 The COSO report defines internal control as a process designed to provide reasonable assurance regarding the achievement of objectives in relation to the following:
• Effectiveness and efficiency of operations
• Reliability of financial reporting
• Compliance with applicable laws and regulations

1.3 Internal control is fundamental to the successful operation and day-to-day running of a business and it assists the company in achieving its business objectives. As indicated above, the scope of internal control is very broad. It encompasses all controls incorporated into the strategic, governance and management processes, covering the company’s entire range of activities and operations, and not just those directly related to financial operations and reporting. Its scope is not confined to those aspects of a business that could broadly be defined as compliance matters, but extends also to the performance aspects of a business. (See Figure 1.)
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-65.png)
1.4 Internal controls need to be responsive to the specific nature and needs of the business. Hence, they should seek to reflect sound business practice, remain relevant over time in the continuously evolving business environment and enable the company to respond to the specific needs of the business or industry.

1.5 It is important that control should not be seen as a burden on business but, rather, the means by which business opportunities are maximised and potential losses associated with unwanted events reduced. Furthermore, successful companies should not allow themselves to become complacent or blinded by their own success. There are numerous examples of companies whose success has been jeopardised by a lack of, or deficiencies in, internal controls.

1.6 At the same time, the cost/benefit equation is also relevant to any internal control system. Cost/benefit considerations should be taken into account both in the overall design of the system and in the context of risk identification, assessment and prioritisation.

#### Function of internal control

1.7 Control is not synonymous with managing and does not constitute everything involved in the management of a company. While it aims to support the achievement of business objectives, and should serve as an early warning system of possible impediments to achieving those objectives, internal control does not, on the other hand, indicate what objectives to set. While it can help to ensure that reliable information is made available for decision-making, implementation and monitoring, and can facilitate assessment and reporting on the results of actions taken, it does not take the place of the management in making strategic and operational decisions. In addition, decisions about whether to act and what action to take are outside the scope of internal control.

1.8 It follows from the above that there are inherent limitations in control. A sound and well-designed system of internal control reduces, but cannot eliminate, the possibility of poor
judgement in decision-making; human error or mistake; control activities and processes being deliberately circumvented by the collusion of employees or others; management overriding controls; and the occurrence of unforeseeable circumstances.

1.9 A sound system of internal control therefore helps to provide reasonable, but not absolute, assurance that a company will avoid being hindered in achieving its business objectives, or in the orderly and legitimate conduct of its business, by circumstances that may reasonably be foreseen. A system of internal control cannot, however, provide protection with certainty against a company failing to meet its business objectives or against all material errors, losses, fraud, or breaches of laws or regulations.

1.10 As noted in paragraph A.4.1 above, no two companies will, or should, have identical internal control systems. Companies and their control differ by industry, size and organisational structure, and by culture and management philosophy. Therefore, while all companies need each of the components (referred to in paragraph B.2.2 below) to ensure adequate control over their activities, each will have a unique internal control system tailored to meet its own circumstances. The management will have to exercise its judgment, driven by the particular needs of the company, to determine the nature of the controls that should be in place and whether they are functioning effectively in achieving the company’s objectives.

### 2.0 Elements of a sound system of internal control
2.1 An internal control system encompasses the policies, processes, tasks, behaviours and other aspects of a company that, taken together:
• facilitate its effective and efficient operation by enabling it to respond appropriately to significant business, operational, financial, compliance and other risks in relation to achieving the company’s objectives. This includes the safeguarding of assets from inappropriate use or from loss and fraud, and ensuring that liabilities are identified and managed;
• help ensure the quality of internal and external reporting. This requires the maintenance of proper records and processes that generate a flow of timely, relevant and reliable information from within and outside the organisation; and
• help ensure compliance with applicable laws and regulations, and also with internal policies with respect to the conduct of business.

2.2 Internal control can be analysed into five inter-related components, which also serve as criteria for the effectiveness of the internal control system in supporting the achievement of the separate but overlapping operational, financial reporting and compliance objectives. This is illustrated in Figure 2. The components are:
(i) Control environment – the foundation for the other components of internal control, which also provides discipline and structure. Factors include ethical values and
competence (quality) of personnel, direction provided by the board and effectiveness of management.
(ii) Risk assessment – identification and analysis of risks underlying the achievement of objectives, including risks relating to the changing regulatory and operating environment, as a basis for determining how such risks should be mitigated and managed.
(iii) Control activities – a diverse range of policies and procedures that help to ensure management directives are carried out and any actions that may be needed to address risks to achieving company objectives are taken.
(iv) Information and communication – effective processes and systems that identify, capture and report operational, financial and compliance-related information in a form and timeframe that enable people to carry out their responsibilities.
(v) Monitoring – a process that assesses the adequacy and quality of the internal control system’s performance over time. Deficiencies in internal controls should be reported to the appropriate level upstream, which may be, for example, senior management, the audit committee, or the board. 
A more detailed description and breakdown of the five components and their relationships is contained in Appendix III.
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-66.png)
2.3 A company’s system of internal control will reflect its control environment, which encompasses its organisational structure.

2.4 The system of internal control should:
• be embedded in the operations of the company and form part of its culture;
• be capable of responding quickly to evolving risks to the business arising from factors within the company and changes in the business environment; and
• include procedures for reporting immediately to appropriate levels of management any significant control failings or weaknesses that are identified, together with details of corrective action being undertaken.

2.5 Internal control procedures should, as far as possible, given the nature of the individual company concerned, be kept simple and straightforward, and have regard to the need to ensure that (a) the costs do not outweigh the benefits and (b) staff at all levels can “buy into” the importance of maintaining adequate control and are not alienated by unnecessary complexity in implementing it.

2.6 There is a direct relationship between a company’s objectives and the components of internal control that are required to achieve them. A graphical representation of this is reproduced in Figure 3. All of the components apply to the three categories of objectives referred to in paragraph B.1.2 above. The third dimension in Figure 3 represents subsidiaries, divisions, or other business units, and functional or other activities, such as purchasing, production and marketing. This reflects the fact that internal control is relevant not only to an enterprise as a whole, but also to parts of that enterprise.
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-67.png)

## 1.2 Business Ethics and Professional Negligence
[5-step process for resolving ethical dilemmas](ulysses://x-callback-url/open?id=y0o_A22DHBf0ZKPus70M_w)
### Worksheet 1.2.1 – A Professional Negligence Case 25


### Worksheet 1.2.2 – Timee Toys Professional Negligence Case 27

## 5-step process for resolving ethical dilemmas

1. Identify the ethical dilemma (common dilemmas)
	- manipulation: Altering data to mislead or create a false impression in others.
	- omission: Removing data to mislead or create a false impression in others.
	- confidentiality: Misuse of, or failure to protect, sensitive or proprietary information.
	- conflict of interest: The interests of one party conflict with those of another.
	- raise the alarm: When reporting violations may bring serious ramifications.
2. Identify the fundamental principle violated (HKICPA)
	- Integrity - straightforward and honest in all professional and business relationships; fair dealing and truthfulness.
		- x knowingly associated with reports, returns, communications or other info (contain false or misleading statement, furnished recklessly or omits or obscures -\> misleading)
	- Objectivity - not compromise their judgement / not proform (bias, conflict of interest or undue influence of others)
	- Professional competence and due care: maintain knowledge and skill, act diligently, exercise sound judgement
	- Confidentiality: refrain from disclose without proper and specific authority or unless there is a legal or professional right or duty to disclose _and_ use to their personal advantage or advantage of 3rd parties.
	- Professional behaviour: comply with relevant laws and regulations and to avoid any conduct that the professional accountant knows or should know may discredit the profession
3. Discover alternative solutions (common solutions)
	- assess the relative severity of the dilemma
	- identify the potential legal issues, parties affected
	- document the ethical dilemma
	- take outsider’s view, seek legal advice, inform proper authorities
4. Recommend a solutions
5. Explain your choice

## The Five Practices of Exemplary Leadership
￼![](Sketch.png)
1. **Model the Way** - set an example for others (clarify value)
	* Clarify values by finding your voice and affirming shared values
	* Set the example by aligning actions with shared values
2. **Inspire a Shared Vision** - boldly and creatively communicate their hopes and dreams, and together with their teams’ desires (understand the needs of your followers)
	* Envision the future by imagining exciting and ennobling possibilities
	* Enlist others in a common vision by appealing to shared aspirations.
3. **Challenge the Process **(problems - innovation), experiment and take risks, treating the inevitable mistakes as important learning opportunities and creating a safe environment in which others can learn from failures as well as from successes. Open to new opportunities
	* Search for opportunities by seizing the initiative and by looking outward for innovative ways to improve
	* Experiment and take risks by constantly generating small wins and learning from experience
4. **Enable others to act **- only solid trust and strong relationships can transform shared visions into reality, helping others to see themselves as capable and powerful - to nurture positive self-esteem (e.g. involve others in decision making and goal setting, and build teams with spirit, cohesion, and a true sense of community), share power and information as well as build the capabilities and capacities of others to be successful.
	* Foster collaboration by building trust and facilitating relationships
	* Strengthen others by increasing self-determination and developing competence.
5. **Encourage the heart** - make sure that people understand in their hearts that what they do matters. They expect the best, share the spotlight and credit for a job well done, celebrating people’s accomplishments in personal and meaningful ways. - Basic human need to be appreciated for who we are and for what we do
	* Recognise contributions by showing appreciation for individual excellence
	* Celebrate the values and victories by creating a spirit of community

## 1.3 Business Acumen - Develop business strategy (Strategy formulation)
### 1. What is strategy and what is not?
#### 1.1. Defining strategy: direction and scope of an organisation over LT
- achieves advantage in a changing environment through its configuration of resources and competencies with the aim of fulfilling stakeholder expectations
- strategic decisions are made under conditions of complexity and uncertainty; wide impact on the organisation and often lead to major change
- strategic positioning -\> sustainable competitive advantage by preserving the distinctive features of a company -\> create a unique and valuable position (perform different activities or similar activities in a different way)
	- operational effectiveness - similar activities better than rival
	- strategy - about how to combine activities to create a unique value

#### 1.2 Overview of steps in formulating strategies
Step 1 set mission and objectives: LT aims broken down into ST, more measurable targets
Step 2 conduct a corporate appraisal: internal and external environment
Step 3 gap analysis: compare forecast performance with strategic objectives
Step 4 strategic choice: generate options and evaluate and decide
Step 5 strategy implementation: corporate, business and functional level
#### 1.3 level of strategies
- corporate: general direction of the whole organisation
- business: tackle particular markets
- operational/functional: different departments
	- marketing: devising products and services, pricing, promoting and distributing them -\> satisfy customer needs at a profit
	- production: location, techniques, outsourcing and so on
	- finance: enough financial resources to fund its other strategies by identifying sources of finance and using them effectively
	- HR: right skills, right quantity, right time -\> overall goals
	- Info systems: admin support and competitive strength
	- R&D: new products and techniques

### 2. Mission and objectives
Strategies are developed to achieve an organisation’s desired outcomes which are incorporated in the organisation’s mission / purpose statement.
- mission (purpose of the business): guides strategic decisions and provide values and a sense of direction
- vision - future
#### 2.1. mission and planning![](Screenshot%202021-10-31%20at%207.27.58%20PM.png)
#### 2.2 stakeholders - legitimate interest
- internal (employees, management)
- connected (shareholders, customers, suppliers, financiers)
- external (the community, government, pressure groups)
#### 2.3 Objective of an organisation
- **economic** - profit maximisation (ST), profitability maximisation, shareholder wealth maximisation
- **non-financial objectives ** - enabling objectives vs personal objectives of managers
	- e.g. market and brand position, global reach, managing exposure to business risks, CSR
- Not for profit organisations

### 3. Corporate appraisal (internal)
Mission and objectives -\> external and internal analyses - formulate strategic position
#### 3.1 environmental analysis 
- org in open systems (receive inputs and provide output)
- 3 concentric layers - 
	- micro-environment: [PESTEL](ulysses://x-callback-url/open?id=qBnk1ml-q25T7-6-KjWqaw)
	- industry or sector: [Porter’s Five Forces](ulysses://x-callback-url/open?id=X6oWD3sHBNLGrlL0a9j8rQ), [Value Chain](ulysses://x-callback-url/open?id=87tROCMiwbmlYUW1ozPoZw)
	- competitors and markets: Porter’s Five Forces

#### 3.2 resources analysis
Resource audit - 9M model: machinery, make-up, management, management information, markets, materials, men and women, methods, money
- pay attention to unique resources and limiting factors

[SWOT analysis](ulysses://x-callback-url/open?id=RDtDO_p5C8wDvoToIzQtgg)
* Worksheet 1.3.1 – Value Chain Analysis
![](IMG_96586463DF45-1.jpeg "strategic management process")

Example 1 - Strategic position analysis: HK Jocky Club
- vision and mission -\> SWOT

## PESTEL
(M12 s.1.1.2.4)
External influences by source, shape the competitive conditions
1. Political: e.g. variable such as licensing by authorities, taxation, TM and IP requirements
2. Economic: cost of labor, capital and energy, demand and supply
3. Social: social attitudes -\> types of customers
4. Technological: technology and technological changes
5. Environmental: GHG
6. Legal: labour laws, health and safety standards, consumer protection laws, taxation laws, environmental laws

## Porter’s Five Forces
(M12 s.1.1.2.1)
Affect industry attractiveness and profitability:
1. Rivalry among industry competitors: five factors determine the intensity of competition between established firms in a particular industry: 
	- seller concentration (#)
	- diversity of competitors (#) 
	- product differentiation (v) 
	- excess capacity and exit barriers (x)
	- cost conditions (scale economies)
2. Bargain power of suppliers (size and scale)
3. Threat of potential new entrants (open to new participants?) e.g. 
	- capital requirements, economies of scale, absolute cost advantages, product differentiation, access to channels of distribution, governmental and legal barriers, retaliation, effectiveness of barriers to entry
4. Threat of substitutes
	- customer insensitive to the price they are willing to pay
5. Bargaining power of buyers
	- 2 types of markets: for inputs (raw materials, components, labour services and financial services by individual firms) and outputs (sale of goods and services by firms to their customers)
	- factors - 
		- buyers’ price sensitive (greater proportion of total cost, differentiated products, competition among buyers, critical)
		- firm’s relative bargaining power (relative cost to each when a transaction is not completed, relative expertise) (size and concentration of buyers, buyers’ information, ability to integrate vertically)

## Value Chain Analysis
(M12 s1.1.2.5)
Sequence of activities that transform raw materials into finished products, with each subsequent key activity adding value to the activity before it.
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-70.png)

2 types of activities in the value chain
- primary activities: 
	- inbound logistics: (getting it) purchasing and supply chain management
	- operations: depend on the type of business, incl. production and administrative duties carried out by employees
	- outbound logistics: (selling it) **distribution** of a product or service rendered by the organisation
	- marketing and sales - advertising and selling strategies designed to make the organisation’s product or service stand out in the market and to consumers
	- servicing - organisation’s policies and strategies for interacting with its client base, even after a sale such as when follow-up warranty work is completed.
- support activities
	- infrastructure - basic foundation of an organisation, incl. strategic and risk management, internal and external relations, financial activities, and governance, among other critical activities
	- HR - employee recruiting, training, skills development, performance management, and promotion
	- Technology development - operational activities e.g. organisation’s info management systems, its design of proprietary technology, and technology related sales
	- procurement - acquiring goods and services critical to the organisation through negotiation strategies, supply chain coordination and database and inventory management

## SWOT analysis
(M12 s.1.1.3.1)
SW (internal attributes)
OT (external environment)

Take into account a wide variety of factors: incl. financial, competitiveness, technological, management, and skills, a macro-assessment of the industry and market within which it operates, and the organisation’s customers and rivals. 

In preparing the SWOT analysis, factors within each aspect must be ranked in order of priority to the organisation so that focus is properly placed when developing specific strategies.


## 2.2 Business Acumen: formulate business decision (strategic decision making)
After evaluating an entity’s objectives, impact of external environment and internal resources, the entity start to **identify potential alternative strategies** to achieve or sustain its objectives.
- Competitive strategies - generic strategies - how to compete
- product-market strategies - where to compete
- growth strategies - organic / internal growth, acquisition (external growth), joint development

![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-71.png)

### 1. Key models for generating strategic options
#### 1.1 [Ansoff matrix](ulysses://x-callback-url/open?id=mxH-J65VyLCdXwo8VIHgPQ) 
- product - market strategies (where to compete)
#### 1.2 [Porter’s generic competitive strategies](ulysses://x-callback-url/open?id=nHm40ao0Mpo1_ocduZL-yg) (vs external)
- cost leadership, differentiation, focus

### 2. Evaluation of strategy options
- suitability - fit the company’s operational circumstances and strategic position? —\> SWOT analysis, its external environment, its mission and objectives and its competencies
- acceptability - stakeholders’ expectations —\> Shareholders - risks and returns; other stakeholders - ethics and corporate responsibility
- feasibility - time and resources -\> can access sufficient finance and resources, deliver results within an appropriate timeframe

### 3. Stakeholder engagement
Managers - increase shareholder value; external stakeholders - social and ethical obligations
#### 3.1 stakeholder management
Critical in many areas of management of a business:
- overall business strategy: meet the needs of the most crucial stakeholders 
- individual projects and initiatives: require the support and input of stakeholders
- identifying their information requirements: require info to help them do their jobs or to reassure them
Examples of stakeholder engagement include:
- investor relations strategy
- staff communications plan
- supplier engagement 
- political and regulator communications and meetings
- customer liaison and customer care
- local community engagement
- media relations
#### 3.2 principles of stakeholder engagement - communication
- create points of contact 
- consultation
- communication - 
- operate at a human level
- plan for stakeholder engagement
- build a relationship
- be willing to listen
- be clear what they want from the engagement
- take responsibility 
#### 3.3 stakeholder mapping
Not enough time 
- prioritize - level of interest and level of power (crucial): 
	- monitor, keep informed, keep satisfied, manage closely

### 4. Decision making with multiple objectives
Clarify the choices on trade-offs in the minds of management and lead to a decision that reaches consensus
- set a single hierarchy of objectives - priority
- each stakeholder sets their own hierarchy of objectives - prioritise each group
- list the consequences of alternatives for reaching each objective - achieve vs not
- rate how well each consequence meets each objective
- weight the objectives

- profits: financial return to investors

- manufactured: human-created tools and machines
- Intellectual: knowledge in the business and its IP
- Human: skills and abilities of its staff
- Social and relationship: within organisation and between the organisation and its external stakeholders
- Natural: use of natural resources and the impact on the natural environment

## Ansoff matrix
(M12 s.1.1.2.6)
![](DraggedImage-2.tiff)
Product - market strategies (where to compete)
- market penetration (current markets, current products)
- market development (existing products, new markets)

## Porter’s generic competitive strategies
![](DraggedImage-3.tiff)
**Cost leadership**: being the lowest cost producer in the industry as a whole
**Differentiation**: exploitation of a product or service that the industry as a whole believes to be unique
**Focus**: involves a restriction of activities to only part of the market (i.e. focus on a segment)
- (a) Providing goods and/or services at lower cost (cost focus)
- (b) Providing a differentiated product or service (differentiation focus)

## 2.3 Business Acumen: implement business solution (strategic implementation)
Strategy implementation is the conversion of the chosen strategy into detailed plans or objectives for operating units, 

The **planning** of implementation has several aspects:
- resource planning
- operations planning
- organisation structure and control system
 A business plan describes: the goals, strategies, and resources of a business
- include a number of sub-plans covering operational, marketing and financial aspects of the business
An implementation of business plans, it is essential to identify the CSFs and KPIs can be used for measurement and feedback purposes


### 1. Critical Success Factors and Key Performance Indicators
#### 1.1 Critical success factors 
a small number of key goals vital to success of the org
- things that must go right
- product features that are particularly valued
Usually expressed in words, not numbers. Characteristics:
- essential to the successful completion of the organisation’s objectives
- expressed as an action
- small numbers to help focus efforts and improve control ≤ 10
#### 1.2 Key performance indicators - performance measures
Role in strategic implementation and business management is summarised by Neely as ‘the four CPs’
- confirm priorities (important)
- compel progress (manager to get it done)
- check position (management monitor the activities - go well or not)
- communicate position (read by other staff and outsiders)
- cash prizes: (bonus payments) -\> compel progress
![](DraggedImage-4.tiff)

### 2. Methods of establishing CSFs and KPIs
3 approaches:
- interview with management (ask)
- business analysis (understanding of business systems)
- collaboration in a multidisciplinary project team (functional experts)
Accounting professional
- should contribute their understanding of performance measurement and also of the drivers of financial performance (e.g. cost and revenues)
- should not reduce all factors to ST financial considerations and therefore lose the LT strategy and the support of non-accountants

### 3. Ways to set KPIs
#### 3.1 Four drivers of financial success
Maximise profitability and/or shareholder value -\> 4 drivers: sales revenue, costs, asset investment, risk
![](Study/QP/Capstone/Doc/DraggedImage-73.png)
#### 3.2 Three steps of the process model
Set for evaluating efficiency and effectiveness of a business process at 3 points:
- inputs: leading indicators that show whether the right things are in place for the process to succeed
- processes: the co-incident indicators that show whether the process is performing
- outputs: the lagging indicators that show what has been delivered
